<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> There are absolute constants $`\eta,c_1\gt 0`$ such that, for every sufficiently large integer <i>n</i>, one can choose <i>n</i> points in the unit square so that every triangle they determine has area at least $`c_1n^{-2+\eta}`$. Thus the almost <i>n</i><sup>−2</sup> upper-bound formulation of Heilbronn's triangle problem is false. The exponent <i>η</i> is fixed but extremely small.

- **C01** (theorem, [source](#stmt-intro-main)): There are absolute constants $\eta,c_1>0$ and an integer $n_0\ge3$ such that $$\Delta(n)\ge c_1n^{-2+\eta} \qquad\text{for every integer }n\ge n_0.$$
  - Explanation: A fixed power improvement in the minimum triangle area disproves the almost inverse-square upper-bound formulation.
- **C02** (bound, [source](#stmt-lat-successive)): For a lattice $L$ of positive rank $s$ and the lengths just defined, $$\det L\le\prod_{i=1}^s\lambda_i\le s^{s/2}\det L.$$ Consequently, the lattice generated by $v_1,\ldots,v_s$ has index at most $s^{s/2}$ in $L$.
  - Explanation: Successively chosen shortest independent vectors control the covolume and the index of their integer span.
- **C03** (bound, [source](#stmt-lat-plane-count)): Let $L$ be a rank-two Euclidean lattice with successive lengths $\lambda_1\le\lambda_2$. If $R\ge\lambda_2$, any translate of $L$ has at most $C R^2/\det L$ points in any ball of radius $R$. If $R<\lambda_2$, the points of $L$ in the ball of radius $R$ centered at zero lie in a line and number at most $1+2R/\lambda_1$.
  - Explanation: The count in a planar lattice changes when the radius reaches the second successive length.
- **C04** (bound, [source](#stmt-lat-plane-covolume)): Let $y\in\mathbb Z^3$ be primitive, meaning that its coordinates have greatest common divisor one. Then $L_y=\mathbb Z^3\cap y^\perp$ has determinant $\|y\|$. More generally, if $\Lambda\subseteq\mathbb Z^3$ is a full-rank lattice of index $I$ and $y\cdot\Lambda=g\mathbb Z$ with $g>0$, then $$\det(\Lambda\cap y^\perp)=\frac{I\|y\|}{g}.$$
  - Explanation: The covolume of an integral orthogonal plane is determined by its primitive normal and the image of the ambient lattice.
- **C05** (bound, [source](#stmt-lat-anisotropic)): Let $R_1\ge R_2\ge R_3\ge1$ and let $t\in\mathbb Z\setminus\{0\}$. The number of triples $u_1,u_2,u_3\in\mathbb Z^3$ satisfying $\|u_j\|\le R_j$ and $\det(u_1,u_2,u_3)=t$ is at most $$C(R_1R_2R_3)^2\bigl(\log(2R_1)\bigr)^2.$$ The constant is independent of $t$ and of the three radii.
  - Explanation: Ordered unequal column bounds give a uniform count for any prescribed nonzero determinant.
- **C06** (bound, [source](#stmt-lat-fixed-det)): Let $\Lambda\subseteq\mathbb Z^3$ have index $I$, let $X\ge2$, and suppose its third successive length satisfies $\lambda_3\le X$. For every $t\in\mathbb Z\setminus\{0\}$, the number of $3$ by $3$ matrices with rows in $\Lambda$, row lengths at most $X$, and determinant $t$ is at most $$C\bigl(\log(2X)\bigr)^2\frac{X^6}{I^2}.$$ The constant is absolute.
  - Explanation: A lattice index produces a saving in the count of matrices with a fixed nonzero determinant.
- **C07** (construction, [source](#stmt-norm-decomposition)): There exist homogeneous degree-$d$ polynomials $f_{ia}\in\mathbb F_r[X]$ (allowing the zero polynomial), for $1\le i\le3$ and $0\le a<T$, such that $$\label{norm:determinant-sum} \mathcal N(X^{(1)},X^{(2)},X^{(3)}) =\sum_{a=0}^{T-1} \det\bigl(f_{ia}(X^{(j)})\bigr)_{1\le i,j\le3}.$$ For each $a$, the three polynomials are obtained from three distinct degree-$d$ monomials by multiplying the first by a scalar that may be zero.
  - Explanation: An alternating norm polynomial decomposes into monomial determinants without division by a factorial.
- **C08** (bound, [source](#stmt-norm-prime-interval)): For every sufficiently large integer $n$, there is a prime $p$ with $n<p\le2n$.
  - Explanation: A prime in a dyadic interval supplies the prime choices and cardinality interpolation.
- **C09** (bound, [source](#stmt-norm-obstruction)): Let $C$ be a matrix of three columns constructed by <a href="#norm:column" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:column">[norm:column]</a>, with labels $\xi_1,\xi_2,\xi_3$. If these labels are distinct, then the residue $\det C\in R_h$ has no integer representative in $[-\tau,\tau]$.
  - Explanation: Distinct labels force the determinant residue outside the small centered interval.
- **C10** (classification, [source](#stmt-orb-diagonal)): Let $C\in M_3(R_h)$ have at least one unit entry. There are invertible matrices $P,Q\in\operatorname{GL}_3(R_h)$ and integers $0\leq b\leq e\leq k$ such that $$C=P\,\operatorname{diag}(1,B^b,B^e)\,Q.$$ Set $D=B^b$, $E=B^e$, and $I=DE$, all as positive integers. The subgroup $$\Lambda_C=\{v\in\mathbb Z^3: v\bmod h\text{ belongs to the row span of }C\}$$ is a full lattice satisfying $$=I, \qquad E\mathbb Z^3\subseteq\Lambda_C. \label{orb:lattice}$$ Moreover, $b\geq j$, for $1\leq j\leq k$, if and only if every $2\times2$ minor of $C$ vanishes modulo $B^j$.
  - Explanation: A unit entry permits diagonal reduction, including singular cases; the source index display is incomplete.
- **C11** (bound, [source](#stmt-orb-orbit)): Under the hypotheses and notation of Lemma <a href="#orb:diagonal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="orb:diagonal">10</a>, define $$\mathcal O_C=\{GC:G\in\operatorname{SL}_3(R_h)\}.$$ There is an absolute constant $c_0>0$ such that $$\|\mathcal O_C\|\geq c_0\frac{h^8}{D^3E^2}. \label{orb:orbit-bound}$$ For a uniformly chosen $G\in\operatorname{SL}_3(R_h)$, the matrix $GC$ is uniform on $\mathcal O_C$. Every matrix in this orbit has the same row span and determinant as $C$.
  - Explanation: A special-linear orbit is large and has a uniform distribution under a uniform group element.
- **C12** (bound, [source](#stmt-orb-conditional)): Let $r,L$ be positive integers with $r\geq2$, $r\mid L$, and $L<B$. Let $\mathcal L$ be a label set, and for each $\lambda\in\mathcal L$, $1\leq i\leq3$, and $0\leq u<k$, prescribe a residue $a_{iu}(\lambda)\in\mathbb Z/r\mathbb Z$. Fix any three labels $\lambda_1,\lambda_2,\lambda_3$, allowing repetitions. Independently for every $i,j,u$, choose $d_{iju}$ uniformly from $$\{a\in\{1,\ldots,L\}:a\equiv a_{iu}(\lambda_j)\pmod r\},$$ and define $C\in M_3(R_h)$ by $$C_{ij}=\sum_{u=0}^{k-1}d_{iju}B^u\pmod h.$$ Let $b$ and $D=B^b$ be as in Lemma <a href="#orb:diagonal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="orb:diagonal">10</a>, and put $\theta=B(r/L)^4$. Then, for every $1\leq j\leq k$, $$\Pr(b\geq j\mid\lambda_1,\lambda_2,\lambda_3) \leq(r/L)^{4j}. \label{orb:tail}$$ If $\theta<1$, then $$\mathbb E[D\mid\lambda_1,\lambda_2,\lambda_3] \leq1+\sum_{j=1}^k\theta^j \leq\frac1{1-\theta}. \label{orb:moment}$$
  - Explanation: Independent constrained digits give a tail bound and a conditional divisor moment even when labels repeat.
- **C13** (construction, [source](#stmt-aux-cap)): Let $q$ be an odd prime and $1\le w\le q$ an integer. There is a set $S\subset\{0,\ldots,w-1\}^3\subset\mathbb F_q^3$ with $\|S\|\ge w^3/q$ such that no affine line contains three distinct points of $S$.
  - Explanation: A translated quadratic surface supplies a cap in the small residue box.
- **C14** (bound, [source](#stmt-aux-random-set)): Let $H\ge1$ be an integer, let $q\ge2000H^2$ be prime, and put $w=\lfloor q/(1000H^2)\rfloor$. Suppose $S\subset\{0,\ldots,w-1\}^3$ contains no three distinct collinear points. The set $\mathcal A$ in <a href="#aux:allowed-shifts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="aux:allowed-shifts">[aux:allowed-shifts]</a> has at least $q^3/2$ elements. For every $a\in\mathcal A$ and every invertible $G_q$, the set $V$ in <a href="#aux:set-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="aux:set-definition">[aux:set-definition]</a> has $\|S\|$ elements, does not contain zero, and contains no three distinct collinear points.* *Moreover, let $x=(x_1,x_2,x_3)\in\mathbb Z^3\setminus\{0\}$ satisfy $\|x\|\le H$, where $\|x\|$ is the Euclidean norm. For any $u^{(1)},u^{(2)},u^{(3)}\in V$, the relation $$\label{aux:short-relation} x_1u^{(1)}+x_2u^{(2)}+x_3u^{(3)}=0 \quad\text{in }\mathbb F_q^3$$ is impossible if $x_1+x_2+x_3\ne0$. If $x_1+x_2+x_3=0$, it is impossible whenever the three columns are affinely independent. Consequently, <a href="#aux:short-relation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="aux:short-relation">[aux:short-relation]</a> is impossible for three distinct elements of $V$.
  - Explanation: Restricted shifts preserve the cap and exclude short integer relations among distinct residue points.
- **C15** (bound, [source](#stmt-aux-weights)): For the distribution in <a href="#aux:set-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="aux:set-definition">[aux:set-definition]</a>, with $s=\|S\|\ge1$, the following bounds hold with absolute constants: $$\label{aux:weight-bounds} \begin{aligned} W(A)&\ll1 &&\text{if the columns of }A\bmod q\text{ are affinely independent},\\ W(A)&\ll q^3/s &&\text{if }\operatorname{rank}(A\bmod q)\ge2,\\ W(A)&\ll(q^3/s)^2 &&\text{for every }A. \end{aligned}$$ If $W(A)>0$, no column of $A\bmod q$ is zero, and its columns are either affinely independent or include two equal columns. For an affinely independent triple with $W(A)>0$, there is no nonzero $x\in\mathbb Z^3$ with $\|x\|\le H$ and $Ax=0\bmod q$.
  - Explanation: Auxiliary inclusion weights are controlled separately for affine independence, linear rank and repeated residues.
- **C16** (bound, [source](#stmt-sam-proportional)): For two sampled columns $u,v$, one has $$\label{sam:pair-bound} \Pr\bigl(\pi(u)=\pi(v)\bigr)\ll (hq)^6N^{-3}.$$
  - Explanation: Uniform lifting bounds the probability that sampled columns project to the same point.
- **C17** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-sam-lifting)): Condition on all three labels and digit columns, hence on $C$. For any set $\mathcal E\subseteq\mathcal B^3$ of integral matrices, $$\label{sam:crt-identity} \Pr(A\in\mathcal E\mid C,\text{labels}) =\frac{h^9}{N^9\|\mathcal O\|} \sum_{\substack{A\in\mathcal E\\ A\bmod h\in\mathcal O}}W(A).$$
  - Explanation: Conditioning on the labels and digits gives an exact weighted lifting identity.
- **C18** (bound, [source](#stmt-sam-weighted-count)): Fix a digit matrix $C$, with row lattice $\Lambda$ of index $I=DE$ and special-linear orbit $\mathcal O$ modulo $h$. For each integer $t$ with $\|t\|\le\tau$, let $\mathcal A_t$ consist of the matrices $A$ whose columns lie in $\mathcal B$, whose projected columns are pairwise distinct, and which satisfy $A\bmod h\in\mathcal O$ and $\det A=t$. Then $$\label{sam:weighted-estimate} \sum_{A\in\mathcal A_t}W(A) \ll (\log(2N))^2\frac{N^6}{I^2}.$$
  - Explanation: The weighted count remains uniform for a fixed small determinant, including the zero case.
- **C19** (bound, [source](#stmt-sam-bad-triple)): Three sampled columns satisfy $$\label{sam:triple-bound} \Pr\bigl(\pi(u^{(1)}),\pi(u^{(2)}),\pi(u^{(3)}) \text{ are pairwise distinct},\ \|\det A\|\le\tau\bigr) \ll (\log(2N))^2\frac{h}{N^3r^d}.$$
  - Explanation: Label collisions and the conditional divisor moment bound the probability of a bad triple.
- **C20** (bound, [source](#stmt-zero-weighted-zero)): For the lattice $\Lambda$ and weight $W$ above, let $\mathcal Z$ be the set of integer $3\times3$ matrices whose columns belong to $\mathcal B$, whose rows belong to $\Lambda$, whose determinant is zero, and whose three projected columns are pairwise distinct. Then $$\label{zero:target} \sum_{A\in\mathcal Z}W(A) \ll \log(2N)\frac{N^6}{I^2}.$$ The implied constant is independent of the fixed digit matrix $C$.
  - Explanation: The auxiliary restriction controls determinant-zero matrices with distinct projected columns.
- **C21** (bound, [source](#stmt-zero-plane)): For a primitive vector $x\in\mathbb Z^3$, put $$\Lambda_x=\Lambda\cap x^\perp,\qquad x\cdot\Lambda=g(x)\mathbb Z,\qquad J_x=\det(\Lambda_x),$$ where $g(x)>0$. Then $$\label{zero:plane-data} g(x)\mid E,\qquad J_x=\frac{I\|x\|}{g(x)},\qquad \frac{g(x)^3}{I}\le h^2.$$ The reduction of $\Lambda_x$ modulo $q$ is the entire plane $$\Pi_x=\{v\in\mathbb F_q^3:v\cdot\bar x=0\}, \qquad \bar x=x\bmod q\ne0.$$ If $\Gamma\subseteq\Lambda_x$ is a sublattice of index $m$, the number of ordered triples of vectors in $\Gamma$, all of length at most $4N$ and spanning a plane over $\mathbb Q$, is at most $$\label{zero:row-count} \ll \frac{N^6}{(mJ_x)^3}.$$
  - Explanation: Primitive null vectors determine plane covolumes and modular row restrictions used in counting.
- **C22** (bound, [source](#stmt-zero-moment)): For $R\ge h$, $$\label{zero:moment-bound} \sum_{\substack{x\in\mathbb Z^3\ \mathrm{primitive}\\ R\le \|x\|<2R}}g(x)^3 \ll R^3 I.$$ In particular, this estimate holds when $R>H/2$.
  - Explanation: A dyadic shell estimate bounds the cubic moment of the image divisor.

        "exact_quote": "Let $L$ be a rank-two Euclidean lattice with successive lengths $\\lambda_1\\le\\lambda_2$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lat-plane-covolume",
        "exact_quote": "Let $y\\in\\mathbb Z^3$ be primitive, meaning that its coordinates have greatest common divisor one. Then $L_y=\\mathbb Z^3\\cap y^\\perp$ has determinant $|y|$. More generally, if $\\Lambda\\subseteq\\mathbb Z^3$ is a full-rank lattice of index $I$ and $y\\cdot\\Lambda=g\\mathbb Z$ with $g>0$, then $$\\det(\\Lambda\\cap y^\\perp)=\\frac{I|y|}{g}.$$",
        "source_id": "canonical:PM-191.1"
      },

        "anchor": "stmt-norm-decomposition",
        "exact_quote": "There exist homogeneous degree-$d$ polynomials $f_{ia}\\in\\mathbb F_r[X]$ (allowing the zero polynomial), for $1\\le i\\le3$ and $0\\le a<T$, such that $$\\label{norm:determinant-sum}\n \\mathcal N(X^{(1)},X^{(2)},X^{(3)})\n   =\\sum_{a=0}^{T-1}\n      \\det\\bigl(f_{ia}(X^{(j)})\\bigr)_{1\\le i,j\\le3}.$$ For each $a$, the three polynomials are obtained from three distinct degree-$d$ monomials by multiplying the first by a scalar that may be zero.",

        "exact_quote": "Let $C\\in M_3(R_h)$ have at least one unit entry.           "anchor": "stmt-orb-orbit",
          "exact_quote": "Under the hypotheses and notation of Lemma <a href=\"#orb:diagonal\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"orb:diagonal\">10</a>, define $$\\mathcal O_C=\\{GC:G\\in\\operatorname{SL}_3(R_h)\\}.$$ There is an absolute constant $c_0>0$ such that $$|\\mathcal O_C|\\geq c_0\\frac{h^8}{D^3E^2}.",
          "source_id": "canonical:PM-191.1"
        }
      ],

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-orb-conditional",
        "exact_quote": "Let $r,L$ be positive integers with $r\\geq2$, $r\\mid L$, and $L<B$. Let $\\mathcal L$ be a label set, and for each $\\lambda\\in\\mathcal L$, $1\\leq i\\leq3$, and $0\\leq u<k$, prescribe a residue $a_{iu}(\\lambda)\\in\\mathbb Z/r\\mathbb Z$. Fix any three labels $\\lambda_1,\\lambda_2,\\lambda_3$, allowing repetitions. Independently for every $i,j,u$, choose $d_{iju}$ uniformly from $$\\{a\\in\\{1,\\ldots,L\\}:a\\equiv a_{iu}(\\lambda_j)\\pmod r\\},$$ and define $C\\in M_3(R_h)$ by $$C_{ij}=\\sum_{u=0}^{k-1}d_{iju}B^u\\pmod h.$$ Let $b$ and $D=B^b$ be as in Lemma <a href=\"#orb:diagonal\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"orb:diagonal\">10</a>, and put $\\theta=B(r/L)^4$. Then, for every $1\\leq j\\leq k$, $$\\Pr(b\\geq j\\mid\\lambda_1,\\lambda_2,\\lambda_3)\n \\leq(r/L)^{4j}.\n \\label{orb:tail}$$ If $\\theta<1$, then $$\\mathbb E[D\\mid\\lambda_1,\\lambda_2,\\lambda_3]\n \\leq1+\\sum_{j=1}^k\\theta^j\n \\leq\\frac1{1-\\theta}.\n \\label{orb:moment}$$",
        "source_id": "canonical:PM-191.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {

Then, for every $1\\leq j\\leq k$, $$\\Pr(b\\geq j\\mid\\lambda_1,\\lambda_2,\\lambda_3)\n \\leq(r/L)^{4j}.\n \\label{orb:tail}$$ If $\\theta<1$, then $$\\mathbb E[D\\mid\\lambda_1,\\lambda_2,\\lambda_3]\n \\leq1+\\sum_{j=1}^k\\theta^j\n \\leq\\frac1{1-\\theta}.\n \\label{orb:moment}$$",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-aux-random-set",
        "exact_quote": "Let $H\\ge1$ be an integer, let $q\\ge2000H^2$ be prime, and put $w=\\lfloor q/(1000H^2)\\rfloor$. Suppose $S\\subset\\{0,\\ldots,w-1\\}^3$ contains no three distinct collinear points. The set $\\mathcal A$ in <a href=\"#aux:allowed-shifts\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"aux:allowed-shifts\">[aux:allowed-shifts]</a> has at least $q^3/2$ elements. For every $a\\in\\mathcal A$ and every invertible $G_q$, the set $V$ in <a href=\"#aux:set-definition\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"aux:set-definition\">[aux:set-definition]</a> has $|S|$ elements, does not contain zero, and contains no three distinct collinear points.*\n\n\n*Moreover, let $x=(x_1,x_2,x_3)\\in\\mathbb Z^3\\setminus\\{0\\}$ satisfy $|x|\\le H$, where $|x|$ is the Euclidean norm. For any $u^{(1)},u^{(2)},u^{(3)}\\in V$, the relation $$\\label{aux:short-relation}\n x_1u^{(1)}+x_2u^{(2)}+x_3u^{(3)}=0\n \\quad\\text{in }\\mathbb F_q^3$$ is impossible if $x_1+x_2+x_3\\ne0$. If $x_1+x_2+x_3=0$, it is impossible whenever the three columns are affinely independent. Consequently, <a href=\"#aux:short-relation\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"aux:short-relation\">[aux:short-relation]</a> is impossible for three distinct elements of $V$.",
        "source_id": "canonical:PM-191.1"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-aux-weights",
        "exact_quote": "For the distribution in <a href=\"#aux:set-definition\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"aux:set-definition\">[aux:set-definition]</a>, with $s=|S|\\ge1$, the following bounds hold with absolute constants: $$\\label{aux:weight-bounds}\n \\begin{aligned}\n W(A)&\\ll1\n   &&\\text{if the columns of }A\\bmod q\\text{ are affinely independent},\\\\\n W(A)&\\ll q^3/s\n   &&\\text{if }\\operatorname{rank}(A\\bmod q)\\ge2,\\\\\n W(A)&\\ll(q^3/s)^2\n   &&\\text{for every }A.\n \\end{aligned}$$ If $W(A)>0$, no column of $A\\bmod q$ is zero, and its columns are either affinely independent or include two equal columns.         "exact_quote": "Fix a digit matrix $C$, with row lattice $\\Lambda$ of index $I=DE$ and special-linear orbit $\\mathcal O$ modulo $h$. <a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Triangle area** ([source](#intro:section)): For three points $p,q,r\in\mathbb R^2$, write $$\operatorname{Area}(pqr)=\frac12\|\det(q-p,r-p)\|.$$
- **Minimum triangle area and extremal function** ([source](#intro:section)): If $P\subset[0,1]^2$ is finite and $\|P\|\ge3$, define $$\Delta(P)=\min_{\{p,q,r\}\in\binom P3}\operatorname{Area}(pqr), \qquad \Delta(n)=\max_{\substack{P\subset[0,1]^2\\\|P\|=n}}\Delta(P).$$
- **Successive lengths** ([source](#lat:section)): For a lattice $L$ of positive rank $s$, choose $v_1,\ldots,v_s$ successively, with $v_i$ a shortest lattice vector outside $\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb R}(v_1,\ldots,v_{i-1})$, and put $\lambda_i=\|v_i\|$.
- **Primitive integer vector** ([source](#pm-h-4)): A primitive integer vector has coordinates with greatest common divisor one.
- **Norm polynomial** ([source](#norm:polynomial)): Define $$\label{norm:polynomial} \mathcal N(X^{(1)},X^{(2)},X^{(3)}) =\prod_{j=0}^{d-1}\sigma^j(\mathcal D).$$
- **Digit column** ([source](#norm:column)): Conditional on $\xi$, choose all $3k$ digits independently and uniformly subject to these requirements, and set $$\label{norm:column} c_i=\sum_{v=0}^{k-1}c_{iv}B^v\pmod h.$$
- **Lifted row lattice** ([source](#stmt-orb-diagonal)): The subgroup $$\Lambda_C=\{v\in\mathbb Z^3: v\bmod h\text{ belongs to the row span of }C\}$$ is a full lattice satisfying $$=I, \qquad E\mathbb Z^3\subseteq\Lambda_C. \label{orb:lattice}$$
- **Allowed shifts** ([source](#aux:allowed-shifts)): We exclude this possibility by defining the set of allowed shifts $$\label{aux:allowed-shifts} \mathcal A= \{a\in\mathbb F_q^3: \\|ma_1\\|_q>3Hw\text{ for every }1\le m\le3H\}.$$
- **Auxiliary set** ([source](#aux:set-definition)): Our auxiliary set is $$\label{aux:set-definition} V=G_q(a+S).$$
- **Affine independence** ([source](#aux:set-definition)): Here and below a triple $u^{(1)},u^{(2)},u^{(3)}$ is *affinely independent* if $u^{(2)}-u^{(1)}$ and $u^{(3)}-u^{(1)}$ are linearly independent.
- **Auxiliary weight** ([source](#aux:weight)): For an integer $3\times3$ matrix $A$, define $$\label{aux:weight} W(A)=\left(\frac{q^3}{s}\right)^3 \Pr(\text{all columns of }A\bmod q\text{ belong to }V).$$
- **Projection** ([source](#sam:box)): For $u\in\mathcal B$, define its projection by $$\pi(u)=(u_1/u_3,u_2/u_3)\in[0,1)^2.$$

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-lat-fixed-det) / [proof](#lat:section): Successive lengths and anisotropic plane counting -> Uniform fixed-determinant count
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-norm-obstruction) / [proof](#norm:section): Field norms and carry-controlled digit encoding -> Obstruction to small determinant representatives
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-orb-conditional) / [proof](#orb:section): Diagonal reduction and independent digit conditioning -> Orbit bound and conditional divisor moment
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-aux-weights) / [proof](#aux:section): Quadratic cap with restricted random shifts -> Auxiliary weights and short-relation obstruction
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-zero-weighted-zero) / [proof](#zero:section): Primitive null vectors and modular sublattice restrictions -> Weighted determinant-zero count
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-sam-bad-triple) / [proof](#sam:section): Exact lifting and collision-conditioned averaging -> Bad-triple probability estimate
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-intro-main) / [proof](#alt:section): Alteration, projective scaling and prime interpolation -> Lower area bound at every sufficiently large cardinality

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I-cap**: A translate of the quadratic surface has a box intersection attaining the average cardinality, with no collinear triple of distinct points.
- **I-main**: The minimum triangle area admits a fixed power improvement for every sufficiently large cardinality.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A-square**: Replace the nonsquare coefficient by an arbitrary coefficient. -> The quadratic form can vanish along a nonzero direction, allowing a line on the surface.
- **A-main**: Treat a finite cap certificate as verification of the improved triangle-area theorem. -> The cap does not establish the combined residue sampling, weighted determinant counts, deletion, and interpolation.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The cap proof constructs a finite quadratic surface, enumerates its translates, and selects a box intersection attaining the average. This realizes the cap lemma; it does not certify the asymptotic triangle-area theorem.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON object with integer q and w.
# PRECONDITIONS: q is an odd prime; 1 <= w <= q.
# OUTPUTS: JSON certificate containing nu, shift, and cap points.
# TIME: O(q^3*w^2 + q^2) exact field operations; primality trial division O(sqrt(q)).
# SPACE: O(w^2 + q) stored integers and points.
# ARITHMETIC: exact fmpz integer arithmetic reduced modulo q.
# FAILURE: invalid input raises ValueError; no approximate fallback.
import json, sys
from flint import fmpz
v = json.loads(sys.stdin.read())
q, w = v['q'], v['w']
if type(q) is not int or type(w) is not int or q < 3 or q % 2 == 0 or not 1 <= w <= q:
    raise ValueError('invalid domain')
if any(q % a == 0 for a in range(2, __import__('math').isqrt(q) + 1)):
    raise ValueError('q must be prime')
squares = {int(fmpz(a)**2 % q) for a in range(q)}
nu = next(a for a in range(1, q) if a not in squares)
best = []
shift = None
for bx in range(q):
    for by in range(q):
        for bz in range(q):
            current = []
            for x in range(w):
                for y in range(w):
                    z = int((fmpz(bz) + fmpz(x-bx)**2 - nu*fmpz(y-by)**2) % q)
                    if z < w:
                        current.append([x, y, z])
            if len(current) > len(best):
                best, shift = current, [bx, by, bz]
print(json.dumps({'nu': nu, 'shift': shift, 'points': best}))
```

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L01** (hypothesis): The triangle-area lower bound is an existence result for sufficiently large cardinalities; it does not specify a numerical threshold. Blocked use: ['Applying the asymptotic guarantee to an arbitrary small point count.', 'Treating the existence statement as a lower bound for every point configuration.']
- **L02** (edge_case): The minimum triangle area uses distinct points, and a collinear triple forces the minimum to vanish. Blocked use: ['Accepting collinear triples in a configuration claimed to have positive minimum area.', 'Retaining repeated projected points when counting the cardinality of the retained set.']
- **L03** (hypothesis): The unequal-column determinant count requires ordered Euclidean radii and a nonzero prescribed determinant. Blocked use: ['Applying this count directly to determinant-zero matrices.', 'Applying the stated bound without establishing the ordered radius hypotheses.']
- **L04** (hypothesis): The fixed nonzero-determinant lattice count requires the third successive length to be bounded by the row-length scale. Blocked use: ['Omitting the successive-length condition in a prescribed-determinant count.']
- **L05** (hypothesis): The diagonal-form and orbit arguments concern the stated prime-power ring and a matrix with a unit entry. Blocked use: ['Applying these conclusions to matrices without a unit entry.', 'Transferring the prime-power argument to an arbitrary composite modulus without proof.']
- **L06** (edge_case): Distinct labels supply the determinant obstruction; repeated labels remain an exceptional case requiring the conditional moment and counting estimates. Blocked use: ['Claiming that the label obstruction alone excludes every small determinant.', 'Discarding repeated labels without the remaining probability estimates.']
- **L07** (hypothesis): The digit estimate keeps the individual digit draws independent even after labels are fixed, and its expectation bound requires the stated restriction on the moment parameter. Blocked use: ['Reusing a digit draw across entries on the grounds that their labels agree.', 'Using the geometric-series expectation bound outside its parameter regime.']
- **L08** (hypothesis): The auxiliary cap construction assumes an odd prime field; its line exclusion concerns distinct points. Blocked use: ['Using the nonsquare quadratic-form construction without the odd-prime assumption.', 'Claiming that the cap line property excludes repeated residue columns.']
- **L09** (hypothesis): The short-relation exclusion for distinct auxiliary residues requires the allowed shift as well as the cap line property. Blocked use: ['Replacing the restricted shift by an unrestricted one while retaining the short-relation guarantee.']
- **L10** (edge_case): The samples share the main and auxiliary random choices and need not be independent after those choices are averaged out. Blocked use: ['Factoring unconditional probabilities as if the sampled columns were independent.', 'Removing the conditioning from the exact lifting identity without averaging correctly.']
- **L11** (hypothesis): The determinant-zero weighted count restricts the projected columns to be pairwise distinct. Blocked use: ['Including proportional columns in this determinant-zero count without a separate argument.']
- **L12** (nonconstructive): The alteration proof selects a favorable random outcome through an expectation estimate; it does not supply an efficient procedure for finding that outcome. Blocked use: ['Presenting the asymptotic existence proof as a practical point generator with a certified running time.', 'Treating a finite cap check as certification of the full triangle-area theorem.']
- **L13** (approximation): The asymptotic comparison constants may depend on the fixed construction parameters but not on the growing prime or the sampled data. Blocked use: ['Replacing an unspecified comparison constant with a numerical bound.', 'Allowing construction parameters to grow while using bounds established with them fixed.']
- **L14** (counterexample_scope): The objections to earlier preprints concern the cited arguments and do not establish impossibility of their asserted bounds. Blocked use: ['Using the objections as a proof that stronger power lower bounds cannot hold.']
- **L15** (approximation): The explicit exponent uses fixed construction parameters and is not claimed to be optimized. Blocked use: ['Claiming optimality of the displayed exponent or of the construction parameters.']

### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 191 (`lean/docs/191.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [A power improvement in the Heilbronn triangle lower bound](../../preprints/A-power-improvement-in-the-Heilbronn-triangle-lower-bound-September-25-2026/main.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> Heilbronn's triangle problem asks how large the smallest triangle determined by $n$ points in the unit square can be. The formalization constructs an unbounded sequence of sizes $n$ and point sets for which every triangle has area at least $n^{-2+\eta}$, for one fixed $\eta>0$. It consequently refutes the proposed upper bound of order $n^{-2+\varepsilon}$ for every $\varepsilon>0$. The linked construction is for an unbounded sequence of sizes; the paper's statement for every sufficiently large $n$ is broader.

Comparator source `HeilbronnTriangle.lean` (1999 bytes, sha256 `9e12bbd748dff7adf89cabd85e9e1c5321415d6827ce125755d13b4e17dcafa3`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section

namespace Problem355

abbrev Point := ℝ × ℝ

def triangleArea (p q r : Point) : ℝ :=
  abs ((q.1 - p.1) * (r.2 - p.2) - (q.2 - p.2) * (r.1 - p.1)) / 2

def pointInUnitSquare (p : Point) : Prop :=
  0 ≤ p.1 ∧ p.1 ≤ 1 ∧ 0 ≤ p.2 ∧ p.2 ≤ 1

def pointsInUnitSquare (P : Finset Point) : Prop := by
  classical
  exact ∀ p, p ∈ P → pointInUnitSquare p

def triangleAreasAtLeast (P : Finset Point) (a : ℝ) : Prop := by
  classical
  exact ∀ p, p ∈ P → ∀ q, q ∈ P → ∀ r, r ∈ P →
    p ≠ q → p ≠ r → q ≠ r → a ≤ triangleArea p q r

def hasTriangleAreaAtMost (P : Finset Point) (a : ℝ) : Prop := by
  classical
  exact ∃ p, p ∈ P ∧ ∃ q, q ∈ P ∧ ∃ r, r ∈ P ∧
    p ≠ q ∧ p ≠ r ∧ q ≠ r ∧ triangleArea p q r ≤ a

def heilbronnD : ℕ := 41

def heilbronnM : ℕ := Nat.choose (4 * heilbronnD - 1) heilbronnD

def heilbronnT : ℕ := Nat.choose heilbronnM 3

def heilbronnK : ℕ := heilbronnT ^ 2 + 1

def heilbronnExponent : ℝ :=
  1 / (100000 * (heilbronnK : ℝ))

def eventualAlmostUpperBound (epsilon : ℝ) : Prop :=
  ∃ C : ℝ, 0 < C ∧ ∃ n0 : ℕ, ∀ n : ℕ, n0 ≤ n → 3 ≤ n →
    ∀ P : Finset Point, P.card = n → pointsInUnitSquare P →
      hasTriangleAreaAtMost P
        (C * Real.rpow (n : ℝ) (-2 + epsilon))

attribute [local irreducible] Problem355.heilbronnT
  Problem355.heilbronnM

theorem heilbronn_power_lower_bound :
    0 < heilbronnExponent ∧ ∃ n : ℕ → ℕ, ∃ P : ℕ → Finset Point,
      Filter.Tendsto n Filter.atTop Filter.atTop ∧ ∀ j : ℕ,
        3 ≤ n j ∧ (P j).card = n j ∧ pointsInUnitSquare (P j) ∧
        triangleAreasAtLeast (P j)
          (Real.rpow (n j : ℝ) (-2 + heilbronnExponent)) := by
  sorry

theorem almost_n_minus_two_refuted :
    0 < heilbronnExponent / 2 ∧
      ¬ eventualAlmostUpperBound (heilbronnExponent / 2) := by
  sorry

end Problem355

end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


There are absolute constants $`\eta,c_1\gt 0`$ such that, for every sufficiently large integer <i>n</i>, one can choose <i>n</i> points in the unit square so that every triangle they determine has area at least $`c_1n^{-2+\eta}`$. Thus the almost <i>n</i><sup>−2</sup> upper-bound formulation of Heilbronn's triangle problem is false. The exponent <i>η</i> is fixed but extremely small.

<a id="intro:section"></a>
### Introduction

For three points $p,q,r\in\mathbb R^2$, write $$\operatorname{Area}(pqr)=\frac12|\det(q-p,r-p)|.$$ If $P\subset[0,1]^2$ is finite and $|P|\ge3$, define $$\Delta(P)=\min_{\{p,q,r\}\in\binom P3}\operatorname{Area}(pqr),
 \qquad
 \Delta(n)=\max_{\substack{P\subset[0,1]^2\\|P|=n}}\Delta(P).$$ The minimum is over all unordered triples of distinct points. In particular, a collinear triple makes $\Delta(P)=0$. The maximum defining $\Delta(n)$ exists: the minimum area is continuous on the compact space of ordered $n$-tuples, it is positive for suitable points on a parabola, and a tuple with a repeated point has minimum area zero.

The original Heilbronn conjecture asked whether $\Delta(n)=O(n^{-2})$; see Roth (Roth 1951, 198) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Roth1951](#ref-Roth1951)]. Two elementary constructions attain this scale. Erdős’s finite-field parabola, recorded in Roth (Roth 1951, Appendix) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Roth1951](#ref-Roth1951)], gives a grid configuration whose triangle determinants are nonzero integers before scaling. Random sampling followed by deleting one point from each small triangle also gives $\Delta(n)\gg n^{-2}$. Komlós, Pintz and Szemerédi improved this to $\Delta(n)\gg(\log n)/n^2$, disproving the original conjecture (Komlós, Pintz, and Szemerédi 1982) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[KPS1982](#ref-KPS1982)]. Their argument represents the small-area triples in a random point set as edges of a hypergraph, removes short cycles, and uses a stronger independence bound to select a large subset containing none of those triples (Komlós, Pintz, and Szemerédi 1982, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[KPS1982](#ref-KPS1982)].

<a id="intro:almost"></a>
The almost $n^{-2}$ formulation, discussed in Zakharov’s survey (Zakharov 2026, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Zakharov2026](#ref-Zakharov2026)], asks whether, for every $\varepsilon>0$, there are constants $C_\varepsilon$ and $n_0(\varepsilon)$ such that $$\label{intro:almost}
 \Delta(n)\le C_\varepsilon n^{-2+\varepsilon}
 \qquad(n\ge n_0(\varepsilon)).$$ We disprove this formulation by a power improvement in the lower bound.

<a id="stmt-intro-main"></a>
<div id="intro:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There are absolute constants $\eta,c_1>0$ and an integer $n_0\ge3$ such that $$\Delta(n)\ge c_1n^{-2+\eta}
 \qquad\text{for every integer }n\ge n_0.$$*

</div>

In particular, $\Delta(n)\ge n^{-2+\eta/2}$ for every sufficiently large $n$. The ratio of the theorem’s lower bound to $n^{-2+\eta/2}$ is $c_1n^{\eta/2}\to\infty$, which contradicts <a href="#intro:almost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="intro:almost">[intro:almost]</a> at $\varepsilon=\eta/2$. Section <a href="#alt:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="alt:section">8</a> gives an explicit, though extremely small, admissible exponent.

On the upper-bound side, Roth (Roth 1951) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Roth1951](#ref-Roth1951)] proved the first $o(n^{-1})$ estimate by a density-increment argument, followed by refinements of Schmidt (Schmidt 1972) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Schmidt1972](#ref-Schmidt1972)] and Roth (Roth 1972a, 1972b) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Roth1972a](#ref-Roth1972a), [Roth1972b](#ref-Roth1972b)]. Komlós, Pintz and Szemerédi (Komlós, Pintz, and Szemerédi 1981) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[KPS1981](#ref-KPS1981)] refined Roth’s analytic method to obtain $\Delta(n)\ll_\varepsilon n^{-8/7+\varepsilon}$. Cohen, Pohoata and Zakharov developed new incidence-geometric approaches, first obtaining $\Delta(n)\le n^{-8/7-1/2000}$ for sufficiently large $n$ (Cohen, Pohoata, and Zakharov 2023, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[CPZ2023](#ref-CPZ2023)], and subsequently proving $\Delta(n)\ll_\varepsilon n^{-7/6+\varepsilon}$ for every $\varepsilon>0$ (Cohen, Pohoata, and Zakharov 2025, Theorem 1.8) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[CPZ2025](#ref-CPZ2025)].

Earlier preprints claim stronger power lower bounds than ours, with unresolved issues in the following versions. Ellmann’s version 12 acknowledges an unproved local uniformity assumption and describes the argument as heuristic (Ellmann 2025, 3) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Ellmann2025](#ref-Ellmann2025)]; the deletion estimate uses this assumption (Ellmann 2025, Theorem 4, pp. 5–6) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Ellmann2025](#ref-Ellmann2025)]. In Agama’s version 13, Theorem 4.1 (Agama 2026, 12–13) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Agama2026](#ref-Agama2026)], counting the center with the boundary points introduces a collinear triple consisting of the two endpoints of a diameter and their midpoint. Omitting the center leaves the every-triple estimate unproved, since the argument estimates triangles formed by the center and adjacent boundary points. These objections concern the cited arguments, not the impossibility of their asserted bounds.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The two congruence conditions

<a id="intro:area"></a>
The proof works first with integer columns $u=(u_1,u_2,u_3)^{\mathsf T}$ in a box of the form $$0\le u_1,u_2<N,\qquad N\le u_3<2N.$$ Such a column represents the unit-square point $\pi(u)=(u_1/u_3,u_2/u_3)$. Three columns forming a matrix $A$ give a triangle of area $$\label{intro:area}
 \frac{|\det A|}{2A_{31}A_{32}A_{33}}.$$ We therefore seek many columns for which proportional pairs and triples with small absolute determinant are sufficiently rare to delete.

The first congruence condition separates most triples before they are lifted to integers. Give each column an independent uniform label $\xi\in\mathbb F_{r^d}$, where $d$ is fixed and odd and the prime $r$ grows. The columns $(1,\xi,\xi^2)^{\mathsf T}$ have nonzero determinant whenever their labels are distinct. This is the finite-field parabola underlying Erdős’s construction recorded in Roth (Roth 1951, Appendix) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Roth1951](#ref-Roth1951)]. Taking the field norm to $\mathbb F_r$ produces an alternating polynomial in three groups of coordinates. We express that polynomial as a sum of monomial determinants and encode it in one designated coefficient of a determinant whose entries are base-$B$ expansions. Large-base control of carries has classical precedents in the progression-free constructions of Salem and Spencer (Salem and Spencer 1942) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[SalemSpencer1942](#ref-SalemSpencer1942)] and Behrend (Behrend 1946) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Behrend1946](#ref-Behrend1946)]; here it isolates a determinant coefficient. Modulo $h=B^k$, with $k$ fixed, distinct labels then preclude a small determinant. Independent choices of digits with the prescribed residues leave enough randomness even when labels coincide.

A common random matrix of determinant one mixes the three rows modulo $h$ without changing their determinant. Conditional on the digit columns, the resulting matrix is uniform on a special-linear orbit. The rows of every integral lift lie in a lattice whose index, together with the orbit size, can be read from a diagonal form. An anisotropic lattice count controls lifts of any fixed nonzero determinant, while a conditional moment bound handles singular digit matrices and repeated labels.

The fixed nonzero-determinant count does not cover determinant zero, where all three rows can lie in one integral plane. We control this case with a second congruence condition. At an independent prime $q$, choose residues from a small portion of an affine quadratic surface with no three points on a line. A carefully restricted random translation excludes short integer relations; a random invertible linear map permits uniform counting. The Chinese remainder theorem combines the two congruence conditions, and uniform lifting supplies integer columns in the box.

For a singular lifted matrix with pairwise distinct projected columns, a primitive integer null vector determines a rank-two row lattice. We sum over those vectors, treating equal residue columns and low rank modulo $q$ separately. This supplies the missing count for collinear projected triples.

The exceptional triples require repeated labels, whose probability provides the saving used in an elementary deletion argument. Thus the improvement comes from the arithmetic distribution of sampled points. Formula <a href="#intro:area" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="intro:area">[intro:area]</a> then transfers the determinant bound to every triangle of the retained point set.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Organization and conventions

Section <a href="#lat:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lat:section">2</a> proves the lattice estimates. Section <a href="#norm:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="norm:section">3</a> constructs the norm polynomial and digit distribution, and Section <a href="#orb:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="orb:section">4</a> estimates its matrix orbits. Section <a href="#aux:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="aux:section">5</a> constructs the auxiliary residues. Sections <a href="#sam:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sam:section">6</a> and <a href="#zero:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="zero:section">7</a> count small determinants, including zero. Section <a href="#alt:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="alt:section">8</a> completes the deletion and scaling argument and passes to every sufficiently large cardinality. Appendix <a href="#app:prime-interval" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:prime-interval">9</a> gives the elementary prime-interval proof used in the parameter choices and interpolation.

Lengths are Euclidean. A primitive integer vector has coordinates with greatest common divisor one. The covolume of a lattice is measured in its real span. The notation $F\ll G$ means $|F|\le CG$; constants may depend on the fixed construction parameters, but never on the growing prime or on the sampled labels and columns. All logarithms are natural.

<a id="lat:section"></a>
### Lattice counts with a prescribed determinant

We prove a uniform bound for integer matrices whose rows belong to a fixed lattice and whose determinant is a prescribed nonzero integer. The lattice may have very different scales in different directions, so the proof keeps track of three separate column bounds. We also record the plane counts needed when the determinant is zero.

All lengths in this section are Euclidean. The determinant of a lattice means its covolume in its real span. For a lattice $L$ of positive rank $s$, choose $v_1,\ldots,v_s$ successively, with $v_i$ a shortest lattice vector outside $\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb R}(v_1,\ldots,v_{i-1})$, and put $\lambda_i=|v_i|$. In particular, $0<\lambda_1\le\cdots\le\lambda_s$. The notation $A\ll B$ means $A\le CB$ for an absolute constant $C$, unless dependence is indicated.

The classical geometry-of-numbers antecedent is Minkowski’s second theorem on successive minima; see Henk (Henk 2002, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Henk2002](#ref-Henk2002)]. Only the weaker fixed-dimensional Euclidean product bound below is needed here, and we include its proof.

<a id="stmt-lat-successive"></a>
<div id="lat:successive" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (Successive lengths). *For a lattice $L$ of positive rank $s$ and the lengths just defined, $$\det L\le\prod_{i=1}^s\lambda_i\le s^{s/2}\det L.$$ Consequently, the lattice generated by $v_1,\ldots,v_s$ has index at most $s^{s/2}$ in $L$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lat-successive"><summary>Proof of stmt-lat-successive</summary>

*Proof.* The determinant of the lattice generated by the $v_i$ is an integer multiple of $\det L$ and is at most $\prod_i\lambda_i$. For the reverse bound, choose orthonormal coordinates whose first $j$ directions span $v_1,\ldots,v_j$ for each $j$. Let $$Q=\{(a_1,\ldots,a_s): |a_i|<\lambda_i/\sqrt{s}\text{ for every }i\}.$$ If a nonzero lattice vector in $Q$ has last nonzero coordinate $j$, it lies outside the preceding span and has length strictly less than $\lambda_j$, a contradiction. The translates of $Q/2$ by $L$ are therefore disjoint. Integrating their indicator functions over a fundamental domain of $L$ gives $\operatorname{vol}(Q/2)\le\det L$. Since $\operatorname{vol}(Q/2)=s^{-s/2}\prod_i\lambda_i$, the result follows. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lat-plane-count"></a>
<div id="lat:plane-count" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Points in a plane lattice). *Let $L$ be a rank-two Euclidean lattice with successive lengths $\lambda_1\le\lambda_2$. If $R\ge\lambda_2$, any translate of $L$ has at most $C R^2/\det L$ points in any ball of radius $R$. If $R<\lambda_2$, the points of $L$ in the ball of radius $R$ centered at zero lie in a line and number at most $1+2R/\lambda_1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lat-plane-count"><summary>Proof of stmt-lat-plane-count</summary>

*Proof.* For the first assertion let $L_0=\mathbb Zv_1+\mathbb Zv_2$. A half-open fundamental parallelogram for $L_0$ has diameter at most $\lambda_1+\lambda_2\le2R$. For each coset of $L_0$, attach such a parallelogram to every point in the ball. The parallelograms are disjoint and lie in a disk of radius at most $3R$ in the affine span. Thus each coset contributes at most $9\pi R^2/\det L_0$ points. Summing over $[L:L_0]$ cosets proves the first assertion. For the second, every lattice vector outside $\mathbb Rv_1$ has length at least $\lambda_2$. The lattice on $\mathbb Rv_1$ is $\mathbb Zv_1$, since a shorter generator would contradict the choice of $v_1$. Counting its multiples proves the bound. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lat-plane-covolume"></a>
<div id="lat:plane-covolume" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Covolumes of integral planes). *Let $y\in\mathbb Z^3$ be primitive, meaning that its coordinates have greatest common divisor one. Then $L_y=\mathbb Z^3\cap y^\perp$ has determinant $|y|$. More generally, if $\Lambda\subseteq\mathbb Z^3$ is a full-rank lattice of index $I$ and $y\cdot\Lambda=g\mathbb Z$ with $g>0$, then $$\det(\Lambda\cap y^\perp)=\frac{I|y|}{g}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lat-plane-covolume"><summary>Proof of stmt-lat-plane-covolume</summary>

*Proof.* Choose $u\in\Lambda$ with $y\cdot u=g$. The map $v\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \OriginalMapsto\EndAccSupp{}}y\cdot v/g$ maps $\Lambda$ onto $\mathbb Z$, has kernel $\Lambda\cap y^\perp$, and splits by $1\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \OriginalMapsto\EndAccSupp{}}u$. Hence a basis of the kernel together with $u$ is a basis of $\Lambda$. The height of $u$ above $y^\perp$ is $g/|y|$, which proves the formula. For $\Lambda=\mathbb Z^3$, primitivity gives $g=1$. ◻

</details>

We next count matrices with unequal column bounds. The difficult case occurs when the third-column radius reaches only one direction of its integral plane; we count those planes through their shortest vectors.

<a id="stmt-lat-anisotropic"></a>
<div id="lat:anisotropic" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Unequal column bounds). *Let $R_1\ge R_2\ge R_3\ge1$ and let $t\in\mathbb Z\setminus\{0\}$. The number of triples $u_1,u_2,u_3\in\mathbb Z^3$ satisfying $|u_j|\le R_j$ and $\det(u_1,u_2,u_3)=t$ is at most $$C(R_1R_2R_3)^2\bigl(\log(2R_1)\bigr)^2.$$ The constant is independent of $t$ and of the three radii.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lat-anisotropic"><summary>Proof of stmt-lat-anisotropic</summary>

*Proof.* The columns $u_2,u_3$ are independent. Choose a primitive normal $y$ to their plane, fixing its sign by requiring its first nonzero coordinate to be positive. Then $u_2\times u_3=a y$ for some nonzero integer $a$, so $|y|\le R_2R_3$. If $\mu_1,\mu_2$ are the successive lengths of $L_y$, the columns imply $\mu_1\le R_3$ and $\mu_2\le R_2$.

For fixed $u_2,u_3$, the determinant equation is $u_1\cdot y=t/a$. Its integral solutions are either absent or form a translate of $L_y$. By Lemmas <a href="#lat:plane-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lat:plane-count">3</a> and <a href="#lat:plane-covolume" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lat:plane-covolume">4</a>, at most $C R_1^2/|y|$ satisfy the first-column bound; the required hypothesis is $\mu_2\le R_2\le R_1$. For a fixed normal $y$, there are likewise at most $C R_2^2/|y|$ choices for $u_2$.

First suppose $\mu_2\le R_3$. There are at most $C R_3^2/|y|$ choices for $u_3$. The resulting bound, summed over normals, is $$C(R_1R_2R_3)^2
 \sum_{\substack{y\in\mathbb Z^3\\0<|y|\le R_2R_3}}|y|^{-3}
 \ll (R_1R_2R_3)^2\log(2R_2R_3).$$ The last estimate follows by splitting the integer vectors into dyadic shells; a shell of radius $Y\ge1$ has $O(Y^3)$ vectors.

It remains to treat $\mu_2>R_3$. Group the normals by powers of two $U,Z\ge1$ with $$U\le\mu_1<2U,\qquad Z\le\mu_2<2Z.$$ Lemma <a href="#lat:successive" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lat:successive">2</a> gives $|y|\asymp UZ$. There are at most $C U^4Z^2$ normals in such a group. Indeed, choose a shortest nonzero vector $z\in L_y$. It is primitive in $\mathbb Z^3$, because division by a common divisor would produce a shorter vector in $L_y$. Its length is in $[U,2U)$, giving $O(U^3)$ choices. For each such $z$, the possible normals lie in $L_z$ and have length less than $4UZ$. If $\nu_1,\nu_2$ are the successive lengths of $L_z$, integrality gives $\nu_1\ge1$, and the preceding lemmas give $$\nu_2\le\nu_1\nu_2\le2|z|<4U\le4UZ.$$ Consequently Lemma <a href="#lat:plane-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lat:plane-count">3</a>, applied at radius $4UZ$, bounds their number by $C(UZ)^2/|z|\le C UZ^2$. This proves the claimed normal count.

For each of these normals, all eligible third columns lie on the line of a shortest vector. Lemma <a href="#lat:plane-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lat:plane-count">3</a> gives at most $1+2R_3/\mu_1\ll R_3/U$ choices, since $U\le\mu_1\le R_3$. Thus the contribution of one dyadic group is at most $$C U^4Z^2\,
 \frac{R_1^2}{UZ}\,
 \frac{R_2^2}{UZ}\,
 \frac{R_3}{U}
 =C R_1^2R_2^2R_3U
 \le C(R_1R_2R_3)^2.$$ There are $O(\log(2R_3)\log(2R_2))$ groups, since $1\le U\le R_3$ and $1\le Z\le R_2$. Combining both cases proves the assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lat-fixed-det"></a>
<div id="lat:fixed-det" class="proposition">

**Proposition 6** (A fixed nonzero determinant). *Let $\Lambda\subseteq\mathbb Z^3$ have index $I$, let $X\ge2$, and suppose its third successive length satisfies $\lambda_3\le X$. For every $t\in\mathbb Z\setminus\{0\}$, the number of $3$ by $3$ matrices with rows in $\Lambda$, row lengths at most $X$, and determinant $t$ is at most $$C\bigl(\log(2X)\bigr)^2\frac{X^6}{I^2}.$$ The constant is absolute.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lat-fixed-det"><summary>Proof of stmt-lat-fixed-det</summary>

*Proof.* Let $V$ be the matrix whose rows are the successive shortest vectors $v_1,v_2,v_3$, and let $\Lambda_0$ be their integer span. By Lemma <a href="#lat:successive" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lat:successive">2</a>, $$m=[\Lambda:\Lambda_0]=\frac{|\det V|}{I}\le3^{3/2}.$$ Since the finite group $\Lambda/\Lambda_0$ has order $m$, each $u\in\Lambda$ has coordinates in $m^{-1}\mathbb Z$ relative to this basis. If $u=\sum_j\alpha_jv_j$ and $|u|\le X$, Cramer’s rule gives $$|\alpha_j|
 \le\frac{X\prod_{i\ne j}\lambda_i}{|\det V|}
 \le 3^{3/2}\frac{X}{\lambda_j}.$$ Thus the map $A\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \OriginalMapsto\EndAccSupp{}}Q=mAV^{-1}$ is injective and takes the matrices under consideration to integral matrices. Their common determinant is $m^3t/\det V\ne0$. If this number is not an integer, there are no such matrices; otherwise Lemma <a href="#lat:anisotropic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lat:anisotropic">5</a> applies.

The $j$th column of $Q$ has length at most $R_j=C_0X/\lambda_j$, where $C_0$ is an absolute constant chosen sufficiently large. These radii satisfy $R_1\ge R_2\ge R_3\ge1$. Moreover, integrality of $\Lambda$ gives $\lambda_j\ge1$, and Lemma <a href="#lat:successive" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lat:successive">2</a> gives $$R_j\le C_0X,\qquad
 R_1R_2R_3=\frac{C_0^3X^3}{\lambda_1\lambda_2\lambda_3}
 \le\frac{C_0^3X^3}{I}.$$ The bound in Lemma <a href="#lat:anisotropic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lat:anisotropic">5</a> is therefore the claimed one. ◻

</details>

<a id="norm:section"></a>
### A determinant obstruction from field norms

We construct random columns modulo a prime power, each carrying a label in a finite field. Three distinct labels will force the determinant residue to have no small integer representative. The construction first expresses a field norm as a sum of determinants, then places these summands at one specified coefficient in a base expansion.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### The norm polynomial

<a id="norm:fixed-parameters"></a>
<a id="norm:polynomial"></a>
Fix $$\label{norm:fixed-parameters}
 d=41,\qquad M=\binom{4d-1}{d},\qquad
 T=\binom{M}{3},\qquad k=T^2+1.$$ Let $r$ be an odd prime. We recall the elementary finite-field facts needed here; see (Lidl and Niederreiter 1994, chap. 2) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[LidlNiederreiter1994](#ref-LidlNiederreiter1994)] for background. In a splitting field over $\mathbb F_r$, the roots of $X^{r^d}-X$ form a field: the Frobenius identity shows closure under addition, subtraction and multiplication, and nonzero roots are closed under inversion. The derivative is $-1$, so there are exactly $r^d$ distinct roots. This field, denoted $K=\mathbb F_{r^d}$, consequently has dimension $d$ over $\mathbb F_r$. Its automorphism $t\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \OriginalMapsto\EndAccSupp{}}t^r$ has $d$th power equal to the identity, and its fixed elements are exactly the $r$ roots of $X^r-X$, namely $\mathbb F_r$. For $t\in K$, the product $\prod_{j=0}^{d-1}t^{r^j}$ is therefore fixed by Frobenius and belongs to $\mathbb F_r$; it is nonzero if $t\ne0$. Choose an $\mathbb F_r$-basis $\beta_1,\ldots,\beta_d$ of $K$. For a group $X=(X_{i\nu})_{1\le i\le3,\,1\le\nu\le d}$ of $3d$ variables, define $$Z(X)=\left(\sum_{\nu=1}^d\beta_\nu X_{1\nu},
             \sum_{\nu=1}^d\beta_\nu X_{2\nu},
             \sum_{\nu=1}^d\beta_\nu X_{3\nu}\right)^{\mathsf T}.$$ For three disjoint variable groups $X^{(1)},X^{(2)},X^{(3)}$, put $$\mathcal D=\det\bigl(Z(X^{(1)}),Z(X^{(2)}),Z(X^{(3)})\bigr).$$ Let $\sigma$ act on this polynomial ring by applying $c\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \OriginalMapsto\EndAccSupp{}}c^r$ to coefficients in $K$ and fixing every variable. Define $$\label{norm:polynomial}
 \mathcal N(X^{(1)},X^{(2)},X^{(3)})
   =\prod_{j=0}^{d-1}\sigma^j(\mathcal D).$$ The action of $\sigma$ permutes the factors, so $\mathcal N$ has coefficients in $\mathbb F_r$. It is homogeneous of degree $d$ in each group. At arguments in $\mathbb F_r^{3d}$, it evaluates to the field norm of the corresponding determinant: $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal N(U^{(1)},U^{(2)},U^{(3)})
  &=\operatorname{Nm}_{K/\mathbb F_r}
       \det\bigl(Z(U^{(1)}),Z(U^{(2)}),Z(U^{(3)})\bigr),\\
 \operatorname{Nm}_{K/\mathbb F_r}(t)&=\prod_{j=0}^{d-1}t^{r^j}.
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed, coefficient conjugation commutes with evaluation at such arguments. Transposing two variable groups changes the sign of each factor in <a href="#norm:polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:polynomial">[norm:polynomial]</a>. Since $d$ is odd, $\mathcal N$ is alternating.

<a id="stmt-norm-decomposition"></a>
<div id="norm:decomposition" class="lemma">

<a id="norm:determinant-sum"></a>
**Lemma 7**. *There exist homogeneous degree-$d$ polynomials $f_{ia}\in\mathbb F_r[X]$ (allowing the zero polynomial), for $1\le i\le3$ and $0\le a<T$, such that $$\label{norm:determinant-sum}
 \mathcal N(X^{(1)},X^{(2)},X^{(3)})
   =\sum_{a=0}^{T-1}
      \det\bigl(f_{ia}(X^{(j)})\bigr)_{1\le i,j\le3}.$$ For each $a$, the three polynomials are obtained from three distinct degree-$d$ monomials by multiplying the first by a scalar that may be zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-norm-decomposition"><summary>Proof of stmt-norm-decomposition</summary>

*Proof.* List the degree-$d$ monomials in $3d$ variables as $m_1,\ldots,m_M$. Expand $\mathcal N$ in products $m_p(X^{(1)})m_q(X^{(2)})m_s(X^{(3)})$. If two of $p,q,s$ coincide, the coefficient equals its negative under the corresponding transposition, and hence is zero because $r$ is odd. For each $p<q<s$, let $c_{pqs}$ be the coefficient of $m_p(X^{(1)})m_q(X^{(2)})m_s(X^{(3)})$. Alternation identifies the other five coefficients with the signs in $$c_{pqs}\det
 \begin{pmatrix}
  m_p(X^{(1)})&m_p(X^{(2)})&m_p(X^{(3)})\\
  m_q(X^{(1)})&m_q(X^{(2)})&m_q(X^{(3)})\\
  m_s(X^{(1)})&m_s(X^{(2)})&m_s(X^{(3)})
 \end{pmatrix}.$$ Enumerate the $T$ triples $p<q<s$ by $a$, and take $$(f_{1a},f_{2a},f_{3a})=(c_{pqs}m_p,m_q,m_s).$$ This argument uses no division by $3!$, so it applies also when $r=3$. ◻

</details>

<a id="norm:distinct-labels"></a>
For a label $\xi\in K$, set $z(\xi)=(1,\xi,\xi^2)^{\mathsf T}$ and let $X(\xi)\in\mathbb F_r^{3d}$ be its coordinates in the chosen basis, so that $Z(X(\xi))=z(\xi)$. The Vandermonde determinant gives $$\label{norm:distinct-labels}
 \mathcal N(X(\xi_1),X(\xi_2),X(\xi_3))
  =\operatorname{Nm}_{K/\mathbb F_r}
        \left(\prod_{1\le i<j\le3}(\xi_j-\xi_i)\right)\ne0$$ whenever the three labels are distinct. Although the basis and the coefficients $f_{ia}$ may depend on $r$, the numbers $d,M,T,k$ in <a href="#norm:fixed-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:fixed-parameters">[norm:fixed-parameters]</a> do not.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Encoding the polynomial in a base expansion

Two prime choices will keep the construction parameters polynomial in $r$. We use the following sufficiently-large form of Bertrand’s postulate; Appendix <a href="#app:prime-interval" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:prime-interval">9</a> gives its elementary binomial proof, following Erdős (Erdős 1932) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Erdos1932](#ref-Erdos1932)].

<a id="stmt-norm-prime-interval"></a>
<div id="norm:prime-interval" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8**. *For every sufficiently large integer $n$, there is a prime $p$ with $n<p\le2n$.*

</div>

<a id="norm:positions"></a>
<a id="norm:matching"></a>
Set $L=r^{10}$. For all sufficiently large $r$, apply Lemma <a href="#norm:prime-interval" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="norm:prime-interval">8</a> with $n=100k^2L^3$ to choose a prime $B$ satisfying <a id="norm:base"></a>$$\label{norm:base}
 100k^2L^3<B\le200k^2L^3,
 \qquad h=B^k,\qquad
 \tau=\left\lfloor\frac{B^{k-1}}2\right\rfloor,
 \qquad R_h=\mathbb Z/h\mathbb Z.$$ For $0\le a<T$, designate positions in rows $1,2,3$, respectively, by $$\label{norm:positions}
 i_a=a,\qquad j_a=Ta,\qquad \ell_a=T^2-(T+1)a.$$ These positions lie in $\{0,\ldots,k-1\}$ and are distinct within each row: they lie respectively in the intervals $[0,T-1]$, $[0,T^2-T]$, and $[1,T^2]$. The only designated positions whose sum is $k-1$ are the matched triples: $$\label{norm:matching}
 i_a+j_{a'}+\ell_{a''}=k-1
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad a=a'=a''.$$ To prove the forward implication, rewrite the equation as $a+Ta'=(T+1)a''$. Reduction modulo $T$, together with $0\le a,a''<T$, gives $a=a''$; substitution then gives $a'=a''$. The reverse implication follows from <a href="#norm:positions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:positions">[norm:positions]</a>.

<a id="norm:column"></a>
A random column $c=(c_1,c_2,c_3)^{\mathsf T}\in R_h^3$ is now defined as follows. First choose $\xi$ uniformly from $K$. For each row $1\le i\le3$ and each position $0\le v<k$, choose an integer digit $c_{iv}\in\{1,\ldots,L\}$. Its required residue modulo $r$ is $f_{1a}(X(\xi))$ when $(i,v)=(1,i_a)$, $f_{2a}(X(\xi))$ when $(i,v)=(2,j_a)$, and $f_{3a}(X(\xi))$ when $(i,v)=(3,\ell_a)$. At every other position its required residue is zero. Conditional on $\xi$, choose all $3k$ digits independently and uniformly subject to these requirements, and set $$\label{norm:column}
 c_i=\sum_{v=0}^{k-1}c_{iv}B^v\pmod h.$$ Every required residue has exactly $L/r=r^9$ possible digits, including the zero residue. In particular a digit prescribed to be zero modulo $r$ is still a positive integer. Since $L<B$, the lowest digit is a unit modulo $B$; thus every entry of $c$ is a unit in $R_h$. Different columns use independent labels and fresh independent digits, even when their labels coincide.

<a id="stmt-norm-obstruction"></a>
<div id="norm:obstruction" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Determinant obstruction). *Let $C$ be a matrix of three columns constructed by <a href="#norm:column" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:column">[norm:column]</a>, with labels $\xi_1,\xi_2,\xi_3$. If these labels are distinct, then the residue $\det C\in R_h$ has no integer representative in $[-\tau,\tau]$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-norm-obstruction"><summary>Proof of stmt-norm-obstruction</summary>

*Proof.* Write $c_{iv}^{(j)}$ for the digit at row $i$, position $v$, in column $j$. Form the integer polynomial $$F(Y)=\det\left(\sum_{v=0}^{k-1}c_{iv}^{(j)}Y^v\right)_{1\le i,j\le3}
      =\sum_{u=0}^{3(k-1)}\gamma_uY^u.$$ For a fixed exponent $u$ and each of the six determinant permutations, choosing the first two digit positions determines the third. Therefore <a id="norm:coefficient-bound"></a>$$\label{norm:coefficient-bound}
 |\gamma_u|\le A_0:=6k^2L^3.$$ Alternatively expanding by rows, $\gamma_{k-1}$ is the sum of determinants of digit rows at positions whose sum is $k-1$. Modulo $r$, all terms with an undesignated position vanish. By <a href="#norm:matching" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:matching">[norm:matching]</a>, the surviving terms are exactly those in <a href="#norm:determinant-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:determinant-sum">[norm:determinant-sum]</a>, evaluated at the three label vectors. Consequently $$\gamma_{k-1}\equiv
 \mathcal N(X(\xi_1),X(\xi_2),X(\xi_3))\pmod r,$$ which is nonzero by <a href="#norm:distinct-labels" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:distinct-labels">[norm:distinct-labels]</a>. In particular $|\gamma_{k-1}|\ge1$ as an integer.

<a id="norm:centered"></a>
The integer $$S_C=\sum_{u=0}^{k-1}\gamma_uB^u$$ represents $\det C$ modulo $h$, because all omitted terms in $F(B)$ are divisible by $B^k$. Since $B-1\ge100k^2L^3$, the coefficient bound gives <a id="norm:lower-carry"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 |S_C|&\le\frac{A_0}{B-1}(h-1)
           \le\frac3{50}(h-1)<\frac h2,
            \label{norm:centered}\\
 \left|\sum_{u=0}^{k-2}\gamma_uB^u\right|
       &\le\frac3{50}(B^{k-1}-1).
            \label{norm:lower-carry}
\end{aligned}$$ It follows that $$|S_C|\ge B^{k-1}-\frac3{50}(B^{k-1}-1)
         =\frac{47B^{k-1}+3}{50}>\tau.$$ By <a href="#norm:centered" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:centered">[norm:centered]</a>, every other representative $S_C+mh$, $m\in\mathbb Z\setminus\{0\}$, has absolute value greater than $h/2>\tau$. This proves the assertion. ◻

</details>

In particular, if $G\in\operatorname{SL}_3(R_h)$ and an integer matrix $A$ satisfies $A\bmod h=GC$ and $|\det A|\le\tau$, then at least two labels of $C$ coincide: multiplication by $G$ preserves its determinant residue. For three independently sampled labels, the probability of such a coincidence is at most $3r^{-d}$ by the union bound.

<a id="orb:section"></a>
### Residue orbits and a conditional divisor estimate

We now describe the lattice of possible rows after a change of coordinates modulo a prime power. The size of the corresponding special-linear orbit will control the probability of a prescribed lifted matrix. Two divisors measure the singularity of the residue matrix; the smaller one has bounded expectation even after all three labels have been fixed.

Throughout this section, let $B$ be a prime, let $k\geq 1$ be an integer, and put $h=B^k$ and $R_h=\mathbb Z/h\mathbb Z$. We interpret $B^k$ as zero when it occurs as an entry of a matrix over $R_h$, but as the positive integer $h$ in counting formulas. The row span of a matrix over $R_h$ is the $R_h$-submodule generated by its rows.

The diagonal reduction below is the prime-power-ring form of Smith’s normal form; see Smith (Smith 1861, Articles 12–16) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Smith1861](#ref-Smith1861)] for the classical integer reduction and determinantal divisors. We include the elementary proof needed here, including singular cases.

<a id="stmt-orb-diagonal"></a>
<div id="orb:diagonal" class="lemma">

<a id="orb:lattice"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Diagonal form and the row lattice). *Let $C\in M_3(R_h)$ have at least one unit entry. There are invertible matrices $P,Q\in\operatorname{GL}_3(R_h)$ and integers $0\leq b\leq e\leq k$ such that $$C=P\,\operatorname{diag}(1,B^b,B^e)\,Q.$$ Set $D=B^b$, $E=B^e$, and $I=DE$, all as positive integers. The subgroup $$\Lambda_C=\{v\in\mathbb Z^3:
       v\bmod h\text{ belongs to the row span of }C\}$$ is a full lattice satisfying $$=I,
 \qquad E\mathbb Z^3\subseteq\Lambda_C.
 \label{orb:lattice}$$ Moreover, $b\geq j$, for $1\leq j\leq k$, if and only if every $2\times2$ minor of $C$ vanishes modulo $B^j$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-orb-diagonal"><summary>Proof of stmt-orb-diagonal</summary>

*Proof.* Move a unit entry into the first position, scale it to $1$, and clear the rest of its row and column by invertible operations. In the remaining $2\times2$ block choose a nonzero entry of least $B$-adic valuation $b$, move it to the first position of that block, and multiply by a unit to make it $B^b$. Every other entry of the block is divisible by $B^b$ in $R_h$, so row and column subtraction clear its row and column. The final entry is still divisible by $B^b$; scaling it by a unit makes it $B^e$ with $b\leq e\leq k$. If the block is zero, take $b=e=k$. This proves the stated form, including all zero-pivot cases.

Invertible row and column operations preserve the ideal generated by the $2\times2$ minors: each new minor is a linear combination of old minors, and the inverse operations give the reverse inclusion. For the diagonal matrix this ideal is $B^bR_h$. Its image modulo $B^j$ is zero exactly when $b\geq j$, proving the last assertion and, in particular, showing that $b$ is independent of the diagonalization.

Write $\mathcal M$ for the row span of $C$. Left multiplication by $P$ does not change the row span, while multiplication on the right by $Q$ is an automorphism of $R_h^3$. Hence $$|\mathcal M|=|R_h\times DR_h\times ER_h|
      =h\frac hD\frac hE=\frac{h^3}{I}.$$ The reduction map identifies $\mathbb Z^3/\Lambda_C$ with $R_h^3/\mathcal M$, which proves the index formula. Since $D$ divides $E$, the diagonal row span contains $ER_h^3$. Right multiplication by $Q$ preserves $ER_h^3$, so $\mathcal M$ also contains $ER_h^3$. Taking inverse images proves the containment in <a href="#orb:lattice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="orb:lattice">[orb:lattice]</a>. ◻

</details>

The preceding description permits singular matrices: either or both of the last two diagonal entries may be zero. The orbit estimate must retain these cases, because zero determinants are included in the triangle problem.

<a id="stmt-orb-orbit"></a>
<div id="orb:orbit" class="lemma">

<a id="orb:orbit-bound"></a>
**Lemma 11** (A special-linear orbit bound). *Under the hypotheses and notation of Lemma <a href="#orb:diagonal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="orb:diagonal">10</a>, define $$\mathcal O_C=\{GC:G\in\operatorname{SL}_3(R_h)\}.$$ There is an absolute constant $c_0>0$ such that $$|\mathcal O_C|\geq c_0\frac{h^8}{D^3E^2}.
 \label{orb:orbit-bound}$$ For a uniformly chosen $G\in\operatorname{SL}_3(R_h)$, the matrix $GC$ is uniform on $\mathcal O_C$. Every matrix in this orbit has the same row span and determinant as $C$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-orb-orbit"><summary>Proof of stmt-orb-orbit</summary>

<a id="orb:stabilizer"></a>
*Proof.* Let $\mathcal G=\operatorname{GL}_3(R_h)$, and let $\mathcal H$ be the stabilizer of $C$ under left multiplication. Conjugation by $P$ identifies $\mathcal H$ with the stabilizer of $\operatorname{diag}(1,D,E)$ and preserves determinants. An element of this latter stabilizer has first column exactly $(1,0,0)^{\mathsf T}$. Its second column differs from $(0,1,0)^{\mathsf T}$ by three entries annihilated by $D$, and its third column differs from $(0,0,1)^{\mathsf T}$ by three entries annihilated by $E$. The respective annihilators have $D$ and $E$ elements. Discarding the invertibility condition gives $$|\mathcal H|\leq D^3E^3.
 \label{orb:stabilizer}$$

<a id="orb:det-image"></a>
For every unit $u\in R_h^\times$ with $u\equiv1\pmod{h/E}$, the matrix $\operatorname{diag}(1,1,u)$ stabilizes $\operatorname{diag}(1,D,E)$. If $e<k$, there are exactly $E$ such units. If $e=k$, this congruence imposes no restriction, so there are $\varphi(h)=(1-B^{-1})E$ of them. Consequently, in all cases, $$|\det\mathcal H|\geq (1-B^{-1})E,
 \qquad
 \frac{|\det\mathcal H|}{|R_h^\times|}\geq\frac Eh.
 \label{orb:det-image}$$ Here $\det\mathcal H$ denotes the image of the determinant homomorphism on $\mathcal H$.

The determinant map from $\mathcal G$ onto $R_h^\times$ has kernel $\operatorname{SL}_3(R_h)$. Applying the orbit–stabilizer formula in these two groups gives the exact identity $$\frac{|\mathcal O_C|}{|\mathcal G C|}
 =\frac{|\det\mathcal H|}{|R_h^\times|}.$$ Reduction modulo $B$ shows that $$|\mathcal G|
 =h^9(1-B^{-1})(1-B^{-2})(1-B^{-3})
 \geq\frac{21}{64}h^9.$$ Together with <a href="#orb:stabilizer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="orb:stabilizer">[orb:stabilizer]</a> and <a href="#orb:det-image" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="orb:det-image">[orb:det-image]</a>, this proves <a href="#orb:orbit-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="orb:orbit-bound">[orb:orbit-bound]</a>, for example with $c_0=21/64$. Every fiber of the map $G\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \OriginalMapsto\EndAccSupp{}}GC$ is a coset of its stabilizer, which proves uniformity. The assertions about row spans and determinants follow directly from invertibility and $\det G=1$. ◻

</details>

We next estimate the smaller divisor $D$ for digit columns. Only the independence and number of digit choices matter here; their prescribed residues may be arbitrary functions of the labels.

<a id="stmt-orb-conditional"></a>
<div id="orb:conditional" class="lemma">

<a id="orb:tail"></a>
<a id="orb:moment"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Conditional digit estimate). *Let $r,L$ be positive integers with $r\geq2$, $r\mid L$, and $L<B$. Let $\mathcal L$ be a label set, and for each $\lambda\in\mathcal L$, $1\leq i\leq3$, and $0\leq u<k$, prescribe a residue $a_{iu}(\lambda)\in\mathbb Z/r\mathbb Z$. Fix any three labels $\lambda_1,\lambda_2,\lambda_3$, allowing repetitions. Independently for every $i,j,u$, choose $d_{iju}$ uniformly from $$\{a\in\{1,\ldots,L\}:a\equiv a_{iu}(\lambda_j)\pmod r\},$$ and define $C\in M_3(R_h)$ by $$C_{ij}=\sum_{u=0}^{k-1}d_{iju}B^u\pmod h.$$ Let $b$ and $D=B^b$ be as in Lemma <a href="#orb:diagonal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="orb:diagonal">10</a>, and put $\theta=B(r/L)^4$. Then, for every $1\leq j\leq k$, $$\Pr(b\geq j\mid\lambda_1,\lambda_2,\lambda_3)
 \leq(r/L)^{4j}.
 \label{orb:tail}$$ If $\theta<1$, then $$\mathbb E[D\mid\lambda_1,\lambda_2,\lambda_3]
 \leq1+\sum_{j=1}^k\theta^j
 \leq\frac1{1-\theta}.
 \label{orb:moment}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-orb-conditional"><summary>Proof of stmt-orb-conditional</summary>

*Proof.* Every prescribed digit set has exactly $L/r$ elements. Since the lowest digit belongs to $\{1,\ldots,L\}$ and $L<B$, every entry of $C$ is a unit in $R_h$.

In addition to the three labels, condition on the complete first column of $C$ and the first entries of its other two columns. If $b\geq j$, Lemma <a href="#orb:diagonal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="orb:diagonal">10</a> implies, for $i,v\in\{2,3\}$, $$C_{iv}\equiv C_{i1}C_{11}^{-1}C_{1v}\pmod{B^j}.$$ The right sides are fixed under this conditioning. Each congruence determines the first $j$ base-$B$ digits of its entry, since all chosen digits lie between $0$ and $B-1$. It therefore has probability either zero or $(r/L)^j$. The four entries use disjoint independent digit draws, even if their labels agree. Their joint probability is at most $(r/L)^{4j}$. Averaging over the additional conditioning proves <a href="#orb:tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="orb:tail">[orb:tail]</a>, including $j=k$.

Finally, the pointwise identity $$B^b=1+\sum_{j=1}^k(B^j-B^{j-1})\,\mathbf1_{\{b\geq j\}}$$ and <a href="#orb:tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="orb:tail">[orb:tail]</a> give <a href="#orb:moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="orb:moment">[orb:moment]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="orb:uniform-moment"></a>
For the parameters $L=r^{10}$ and $B\leq200k^2L^3$ of the digit construction, we have $$\theta\leq200k^2r^{-6}.$$ Thus, for fixed $k$ and all sufficiently large $r$, $$\mathbb E[D\mid\lambda_1,\lambda_2,\lambda_3]\leq2
 \quad\text{for every fixed triple of labels.}
 \label{orb:uniform-moment}$$ In particular, suppose the labels are independent and uniform in a set of size $r^d$, and let $\mathcal E$ be the event that at least two labels coincide. The union bound and conditioning on the labels yield <a id="orb:collision-moment"></a>$$\mathbb E[D\mathbf1_{\mathcal E}]
 \leq2\Pr(\mathcal E)\leq6r^{-d}.
 \label{orb:collision-moment}$$ This is the estimate needed when the digit obstruction restricts a small determinant to triples with repeated labels.

<a id="aux:section"></a>
### An auxiliary cap and its inclusion probabilities

The determinant obstruction at the main modulus leaves triples with repeated labels. We now impose an independent restriction at a second prime. This restriction excludes short integer relations among three distinct residue columns and gives bounds for the probability of each remaining triple, including triples with equal residue columns.

<a id="aux:parameters"></a>
Retain the main modulus $h\ge 2$, and choose a prime $q$ with $$\label{aux:parameters}
 H=h^2,\qquad h^{100}<q\le 2h^{100},\qquad
 w=\left\lfloor\frac{q}{1000H^2}\right\rfloor.$$ Lemma <a href="#norm:prime-interval" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="norm:prime-interval">8</a> supplies such a prime for all sufficiently large $h$. These choices imply $q\ge 2000H^2$, and hence $$\frac{q}{2000H^2}\le w\le\frac{q}{1000H^2}.$$ We identify the integers $0,\ldots,w-1$ with their residues in $\mathbb F_q$.

The following construction uses an affine part of a classical elliptic quadric, a cap with no three collinear points; see Barlotti (Barlotti 1956, 248) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Barlotti1956](#ref-Barlotti1956)]. The proof also gives the required intersection with a small box.

<a id="stmt-aux-cap"></a>
<div id="aux:cap" class="lemma">

**Lemma 13**. *Let $q$ be an odd prime and $1\le w\le q$ an integer. There is a set $S\subset\{0,\ldots,w-1\}^3\subset\mathbb F_q^3$ with $|S|\ge w^3/q$ such that no affine line contains three distinct points of $S$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-aux-cap"><summary>Proof of stmt-aux-cap</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a nonsquare $\nu\in\mathbb F_q$ and put $$Q(x,y)=x^2-\nu y^2,\qquad
 \Gamma=\{(x,y,Q(x,y)):x,y\in\mathbb F_q\}.$$ The form $Q$ vanishes only at $(0,0)$. On a line with nonzero direction $(v_1,v_2)$ in its first two coordinates, the equation defining $\Gamma$ is a quadratic equation in the line parameter with nonzero leading coefficient $Q(v_1,v_2)$. Such a line meets $\Gamma$ at most twice. A line with direction $(0,0,v_3)$ meets $\Gamma$ exactly once. Thus $\Gamma$, and every translate of it, has the asserted line property.

For a uniform $b\in\mathbb F_q^3$, every point belongs to $b+\Gamma$ with probability $|\Gamma|/q^3=1/q$. Therefore the average of $|(b+\Gamma)\cap\{0,\ldots,w-1\}^3|$ is $w^3/q$. Choose a translate attaining at least this average and take its intersection with the box. ◻

</details>

Fix one such set $S$ for the parameters in <a href="#aux:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="aux:parameters">[aux:parameters]</a>, and write $s=|S|$. In particular, <a id="aux:cap-size"></a>$$\label{aux:cap-size}
 s\ge\frac{w^3}{q}\ge\frac{q^2}{2000^3H^6}.$$ The set $S$ is fixed before any random choices are made.

<a id="aux:allowed-shifts"></a>
<a id="aux:set-definition"></a>
For $t\in\mathbb F_q$, write $\|t\|_q$ for the least absolute value of an integer representative of $t$. Three distinct cap points already exclude nontrivial relations over $\mathbb F_q$ whose coefficients sum to zero. For a shift $a\in\mathbb F_q^3$, a relation among three points of $a+S$ with integer coefficients $|x_i|\le H$ and nonzero sum $m=x_1+x_2+x_3$ would force $\|ma_1\|_q\le3Hw$. We exclude this possibility by defining the set of allowed shifts $$\label{aux:allowed-shifts}
 \mathcal A=
 \{a\in\mathbb F_q^3:
       \|ma_1\|_q>3Hw\text{ for every }1\le m\le3H\}.$$ Choose $a$ uniformly from $\mathcal A$, and independently choose $G_q$ uniformly from $\operatorname{GL}_3(\mathbb F_q)$. Our auxiliary set is $$\label{aux:set-definition}
 V=G_q(a+S).$$ Here and below a triple $u^{(1)},u^{(2)},u^{(3)}$ is *affinely independent* if $u^{(2)}-u^{(1)}$ and $u^{(3)}-u^{(1)}$ are linearly independent. This allows the three columns to have linear rank two.

<a id="stmt-aux-random-set"></a>
<div id="aux:random-set" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14**. *Let $H\ge1$ be an integer, let $q\ge2000H^2$ be prime, and put $w=\lfloor q/(1000H^2)\rfloor$. Suppose $S\subset\{0,\ldots,w-1\}^3$ contains no three distinct collinear points. The set $\mathcal A$ in <a href="#aux:allowed-shifts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="aux:allowed-shifts">[aux:allowed-shifts]</a> has at least $q^3/2$ elements. For every $a\in\mathcal A$ and every invertible $G_q$, the set $V$ in <a href="#aux:set-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="aux:set-definition">[aux:set-definition]</a> has $|S|$ elements, does not contain zero, and contains no three distinct collinear points.*

<a id="aux:short-relation"></a>
*Moreover, let $x=(x_1,x_2,x_3)\in\mathbb Z^3\setminus\{0\}$ satisfy $|x|\le H$, where $|x|$ is the Euclidean norm. For any $u^{(1)},u^{(2)},u^{(3)}\in V$, the relation $$\label{aux:short-relation}
 x_1u^{(1)}+x_2u^{(2)}+x_3u^{(3)}=0
 \quad\text{in }\mathbb F_q^3$$ is impossible if $x_1+x_2+x_3\ne0$. If $x_1+x_2+x_3=0$, it is impossible whenever the three columns are affinely independent. Consequently, <a href="#aux:short-relation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="aux:short-relation">[aux:short-relation]</a> is impossible for three distinct elements of $V$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-aux-random-set"><summary>Proof of stmt-aux-random-set</summary>

*Proof.* For each integer $1\le m\le3H<q$, multiplication by $m$ permutes $\mathbb F_q$. Since $3Hw<q/2$, a uniform shift violates the condition for this $m$ with probability $(6Hw+1)/q$. The union bound gives $$\Pr(a\notin\mathcal A)
 \le\frac{3H(6Hw+1)}{q}
 \le\frac{18}{1000}+\frac{3}{2000H}<\frac12.$$ Thus the required uniform choice of $a$ is defined. Invertible affine maps preserve cardinality and the line property. If $a+v=0$ for some $v\in S$, then $\|a_1\|_q\le w-1\le3Hw$, contrary to the condition with $m=1$. Hence $0\notin V$.
