Write $u^{(i)}=G_q(a+v^{(i)})$ with $v^{(i)}\in S$, and let $m=x_1+x_2+x_3$. If $m\ne0$, then $1\le |m|\le3H$. Applying $G_q^{-1}$ to <a href="#aux:short-relation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="aux:short-relation">[aux:short-relation]</a> and taking first coordinates gives $$ma_1=-\sum_{i=1}^3x_i v^{(i)}_1.$$ The integer on the right, using the representatives $0\le v^{(i)}_1<w$, has absolute value at most $3H(w-1)$. Since $\|ma_1\|_q=\||m|a_1\|_q$, this contradicts <a href="#aux:allowed-shifts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="aux:allowed-shifts">[aux:allowed-shifts]</a>.

If $m=0$, the coefficients in <a href="#aux:short-relation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="aux:short-relation">[aux:short-relation]</a> have sum zero also in $\mathbb F_q$. They are not all zero there, since $x\ne0$ and $|x_i|\le H<q$. Such a relation contradicts affine independence: substituting $x_3=-x_1-x_2$ would give a nontrivial linear relation between $u^{(1)}-u^{(3)}$ and $u^{(2)}-u^{(3)}$. Finally, three distinct elements of $V$ are affinely independent by the line property. ◻

</details>

The restricted shift excludes short relations. We next show that this restriction costs only a constant in the inclusion probability of an affinely independent triple, and obtain the weaker bounds needed when residue columns coincide.

<a id="aux:weight"></a>
For an integer $3\times3$ matrix $A$, define $$\label{aux:weight}
 W(A)=\left(\frac{q^3}{s}\right)^3
       \Pr(\text{all columns of }A\bmod q\text{ belong to }V).$$ The probability is over $a$ and $G_q$; $S$ remains fixed.

<a id="stmt-aux-weights"></a>
<div id="aux:weights" class="proposition">

**Proposition 15**. *For the distribution in <a href="#aux:set-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="aux:set-definition">[aux:set-definition]</a>, with $s=|S|\ge1$, the following bounds hold with absolute constants: <a id="aux:weight-bounds"></a>$$\label{aux:weight-bounds}
 \begin{aligned}
 W(A)&\ll1
   &&\text{if the columns of }A\bmod q\text{ are affinely independent},\\
 W(A)&\ll q^3/s
   &&\text{if }\operatorname{rank}(A\bmod q)\ge2,\\
 W(A)&\ll(q^3/s)^2
   &&\text{for every }A.
 \end{aligned}$$ If $W(A)>0$, no column of $A\bmod q$ is zero, and its columns are either affinely independent or include two equal columns. For an affinely independent triple with $W(A)>0$, there is no nonzero $x\in\mathbb Z^3$ with $|x|\le H$ and $Ax=0\bmod q$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-aux-weights"><summary>Proof of stmt-aux-weights</summary>

*Proof.* First replace the allowed shift by an unconditional uniform $a\in\mathbb F_q^3$. Under this replacement the map $v\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \OriginalMapsto\EndAccSupp{}}G_qv+G_qa$ is uniform in the affine group: conditional on $G_q$, its translation $G_qa$ is uniform. Conditioning back on $a\in\mathcal A$ increases the probability of any event by at most two, by Lemma <a href="#aux:random-set" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="aux:random-set">14</a>.

The affine group acts transitively on the ordered affinely independent triples in $\mathbb F_q^3$, whose number is $$q^3(q^3-1)(q^3-q).$$ There are $s(s-1)(s-2)$ such triples in $S$. Therefore, for an affinely independent triple of columns, $$W(A)\le
 2\frac{q^9}{s^3}
 \frac{s(s-1)(s-2)}{q^3(q^3-1)(q^3-q)}\ll1.$$

For the other two bounds, fix any allowed $a$ and put $S_a=a+S$. If two specified target columns are linearly independent, their inverse images under a uniform $G_q$ are uniform among the $(q^3-1)(q^3-q)$ ordered independent pairs. At most $s^2$ of these pairs belong to $S_a^2$. Requiring all three columns to belong to $G_qS_a$ can only reduce this probability. Consequently, $$W(A)\le\frac{q^9}{s^3}\frac{s^2}{(q^3-1)(q^3-q)}
 \ll\frac{q^3}{s}$$ whenever the target matrix has rank at least two. This estimate holds for each allowed shift, so also after averaging over $a$.

If any target column is zero, its inclusion probability is zero. Otherwise fix one target column. Its inverse image under $G_q$ is uniform among the $q^3-1$ nonzero vectors, and $S_a$ consists of $s$ nonzero vectors. Thus $$W(A)\le\frac{q^9}{s^3}\frac{s}{q^3-1}
 \ll\left(\frac{q^3}{s}\right)^2.$$ This also covers all patterns of equal columns. The remaining assertions follow from the line property and short-relation conclusion in Lemma <a href="#aux:random-set" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="aux:random-set">14</a>. ◻

</details>

In the sampling construction, choose $(a,G_q)$ independently of all labels, all digit choices, and the random change of coordinates at the main modulus. Given $V$, choose the auxiliary residues of the sampled columns independently and uniformly from $V$. Since $|V|=s$ for every outcome, the probability of any prescribed ordered triple of auxiliary residues is <a id="aux:normalized-probability"></a>$$\label{aux:normalized-probability}
 \frac{\Pr(\text{all three residues belong to }V)}{s^3}
 =\frac{W(A)}{q^9},$$ where $A$ is any integer lift of that triple. This identity includes repeated residues and is unchanged on conditioning on any main-modulus data. It is the normalization used when the two residue conditions are combined by the Chinese remainder theorem.

<a id="sam:section"></a>
### Sampling integral columns

We now combine the two residue constructions and lift them to integral columns. The purpose of this section is to express the probability of a small determinant as a weighted lattice count. The main and auxiliary changes of coordinates are shared by all samples; fresh labels, digits, auxiliary columns, and lifts are chosen for each sample.

Set <a id="sam:box"></a>$$\label{sam:box}
 N=(hq)^{10},\qquad
 \mathcal B=\bigl([0,N)^2\times[N,2N)\bigr)\cap\mathbb Z^3.$$ For $u\in\mathcal B$, define its projection by $$\pi(u)=(u_1/u_3,u_2/u_3)\in[0,1)^2.$$ Choose $G_h$ uniformly in $\operatorname{SL}_3(\mathbb Z/h\mathbb Z)$, independently of the auxiliary set $V$. Conditional on $(G_h,V)$, sample columns $u\in\mathcal B$ independently as follows:

1.  Draw a label uniformly from $K$ and its digit column $c$ by the main-modulus construction, using fresh independent digit choices. Prescribe $u\bmod h=G_hc$.

2.  Independently choose $u\bmod q$ uniformly from $V$.

3.  Among columns with those residues, choose $u$ uniformly in $\mathcal B$.

Since $h$ and $q$ are coprime and $hq$ divides $N$, each pair of prescribed residues has exactly $(N/(hq))^3$ lifts. This also shows that the sampling rule is defined for every outcome of the residue choices. The samples need not be independent after averaging over $(G_h,V)$; all estimates below retain the shared choices.

<a id="stmt-sam-proportional"></a>
<div id="sam:proportional" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16** (Proportional pairs). *For two sampled columns $u,v$, one has <a id="sam:pair-bound"></a>$$\label{sam:pair-bound}
 \Pr\bigl(\pi(u)=\pi(v)\bigr)\ll (hq)^6N^{-3}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sam-proportional"><summary>Proof of stmt-sam-proportional</summary>

*Proof.* Equality of the projections is equivalent to proportionality of $u$ and $v$, because their third coordinates are positive. Every column in $\mathcal B$ is a positive integer multiple of a unique primitive integral vector $z$ with $z_3>0$. The number of its multiples in $\mathcal B$ is at most $\sqrt6N/|z|$. Therefore the total number of ordered proportional pairs in $\mathcal B^2$, including equal columns, is at most $$6N^2\sum_{0<|z|\le\sqrt6N}|z|^{-2}\ll N^3.$$ For the last estimate, dyadic shells of radius $R$ contain $O(R^3)$ integral vectors and contribute $O(R)$ to the sum.

Condition on both columns’ residues at both moduli and on the shared random choices. Their lifts are independent, each uniform on exactly $(N/(hq))^3$ columns. The preceding count bounds the number of proportional pairs within any two such sets of lifts. Dividing by $(N/(hq))^6$ and averaging proves the claim. ◻

</details>

For three samples, write $A=(u^{(1)},u^{(2)},u^{(3)})$ for the integral matrix of columns, and $C=(c^{(1)},c^{(2)},c^{(3)})$ for their digit matrix before multiplication by $G_h$. With $C$ fixed, use Lemma <a href="#orb:diagonal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="orb:diagonal">10</a> to define $D,E,I=DE$ and the row lattice $\Lambda=\Lambda_C$, and write $\mathcal O=\mathcal O_C$ for the orbit in Lemma <a href="#orb:orbit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="orb:orbit">11</a>. Thus $\Lambda$ is the inverse image in $\mathbb Z^3$ of the row span of $C$ modulo $h$, and $\mathcal O=\{GC:G\in\operatorname{SL}_3(\mathbb Z/h\mathbb Z)\}$. Recall also the auxiliary weight from <a href="#aux:weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="aux:weight">[aux:weight]</a>: $$W(A)=\left(\frac{q^3}{s}\right)^3
 \Pr\bigl(u^{(1)},u^{(2)},u^{(3)}\bmod q\in V\bigr).$$ Here the probability defining $W$ averages only over the auxiliary choices.

<a id="stmt-sam-lifting"></a>
<div id="sam:lifting" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17** (Exact lifting identity). *Condition on all three labels and digit columns, hence on $C$. For any set $\mathcal E\subseteq\mathcal B^3$ of integral matrices, <a id="sam:crt-identity"></a>$$\label{sam:crt-identity}
 \Pr(A\in\mathcal E\mid C,\text{labels})
 =\frac{h^9}{N^9|\mathcal O|}
   \sum_{\substack{A\in\mathcal E\\ A\bmod h\in\mathcal O}}W(A).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sam-lifting"><summary>Proof of stmt-sam-lifting</summary>

*Proof.* The matrix $G_hC$ is uniform on $\mathcal O$, since every point of an orbit has the same number of group elements mapping $C$ to it. Given $V$, the three auxiliary residues are independent and uniform on its $s$ elements. Consequently, for each specified ordered triple of auxiliary residues, its probability is its inclusion probability in $V$, divided by $s^3$. This remains true when some residues coincide. Independence of the main and auxiliary choices and the exact number of lifts give, for each fixed integral $A$ with $A\bmod h\in\mathcal O$, the conditional probability $$\frac{1}{|\mathcal O|}\,
 \frac{\Pr(A\bmod q\text{ has all columns in }V)}{s^3}
 \left(\frac{hq}{N}\right)^9
 =\frac{h^9}{N^9|\mathcal O|}W(A).$$ For other matrices the probability is zero. Summation proves the identity. ◻

</details>

We call a sampled triple *bad* if its projected points are pairwise distinct and $|\det A|\le\tau$. Every matrix in $\mathcal O$ has determinant $\det C$ modulo $h$. Since $2\tau<h$, at most one integer $t\in[-\tau,\tau]$ can occur as the determinant of a bad triple with $A\bmod h\in\mathcal O$. If the labels are all distinct, Lemma <a href="#norm:obstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="norm:obstruction">9</a> rules out every such $t$.

The following estimate handles the one remaining determinant value. Its nonzero case follows immediately from the lattice count; the zero case requires the auxiliary construction and is proved in the next section.

<a id="stmt-sam-weighted-count"></a>
<div id="sam:weighted-count" class="proposition">

**Proposition 18** (Weighted count at a fixed determinant). *Fix a digit matrix $C$, with row lattice $\Lambda$ of index $I=DE$ and special-linear orbit $\mathcal O$ modulo $h$. For each integer $t$ with $|t|\le\tau$, let $\mathcal A_t$ consist of the matrices $A$ whose columns lie in $\mathcal B$, whose projected columns are pairwise distinct, and which satisfy $A\bmod h\in\mathcal O$ and $\det A=t$. Then <a id="sam:weighted-estimate"></a>$$\label{sam:weighted-estimate}
 \sum_{A\in\mathcal A_t}W(A)
 \ll (\log(2N))^2\frac{N^6}{I^2}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sam-weighted-count"><summary>Proof of stmt-sam-weighted-count</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose first that $t\ne0$. Since $0<|t|\le\tau<q$, the reduction of $A$ modulo $q$ has rank three. Its columns are therefore affinely independent, and Proposition <a href="#aux:weights" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="aux:weights">15</a> gives $W(A)\ll1$. Every row of $A$ belongs to $\Lambda$ and has norm less than $4N$. Moreover $E\mathbb Z^3\subseteq\Lambda$ gives $\lambda_3(\Lambda)\le E\le h<4N$. Proposition <a href="#lat:fixed-det" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lat:fixed-det">6</a>, applied with $X=4N$, proves the estimate.

For $t=0$, Proposition <a href="#zero:weighted-zero" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="zero:weighted-zero">20</a> below proves the stronger bound $O(\log(2N)N^6/I^2)$ for all matrices with rows in $\Lambda$ and pairwise distinct projected columns. The orbit restriction only reduces this set, so it gives the claimed estimate as well. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-sam-bad-triple"></a>
<div id="sam:bad-triple" class="corollary">

**Corollary 19** (Probability of a bad triple). *Three sampled columns satisfy <a id="sam:triple-bound"></a>$$\label{sam:triple-bound}
 \Pr\bigl(\pi(u^{(1)}),\pi(u^{(2)}),\pi(u^{(3)})
       \text{ are pairwise distinct},\ |\det A|\le\tau\bigr)
 \ll (\log(2N))^2\frac{h}{N^3r^d}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sam-bad-triple"><summary>Proof of stmt-sam-bad-triple</summary>

*Proof.* Conditional on the labels and digit matrix $C$, the probability is zero when all labels are distinct. Otherwise Lemma <a href="#sam:lifting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sam:lifting">17</a> and Proposition <a href="#sam:weighted-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sam:weighted-count">18</a>, together with the bound $|\mathcal O|\gg h^8/(D^3E^2)$ from <a href="#orb:orbit-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="orb:orbit-bound">[orb:orbit-bound]</a>, bound it by $$\frac{h^9}{N^9}\frac{D^3E^2}{h^8}
 (\log(2N))^2\frac{N^6}{D^2E^2}
 \ll (\log(2N))^2\frac{hD}{N^3}.$$ Let $F$ be the event that two of the three labels agree. The uniform conditional moment estimate <a href="#orb:uniform-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="orb:uniform-moment">[orb:uniform-moment]</a> gives $$\mathbb E\bigl[D\mathbf1_F\bigr]
 =\mathbb E_{\mathrm{labels}}
   \bigl[\mathbf1_F\mathbb E(D\mid\mathrm{labels})\bigr]
 \ll\Pr(F)\le 3r^{-d}.$$ This step uses the moment estimate for every fixed label triple, including repeated labels; the corresponding digit draws remain independent. Averaging the conditional bound proves the corollary. ◻

</details>

<a id="zero:section"></a>
### Counting determinant-zero triples

The remaining part of the weighted count concerns collinear projected points. We organize these triples by their integral linear relation. The auxiliary modulus excludes short relations when the three residues are affinely independent. When two residues coincide, the equality usually imposes an additional lattice condition; the relations for which it does not are sufficiently sparse.

Keep the digit matrix $C$ fixed, and write $\Lambda=\Lambda_C$ for its row lattice from Lemma <a href="#orb:diagonal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="orb:diagonal">10</a>. Write $$h=B^k,\qquad D=B^b,\qquad E=B^e,\qquad I=DE,
 \qquad 0\le b\le e\le k.$$ Thus $C$ has diagonal form $(1,D,E)$ over $\mathbb Z/h\mathbb Z$, $[\mathbb Z^3:\Lambda]=I$, and $E\mathbb Z^3\subseteq\Lambda$. Recall also $H=h^2$, $q>h^{100}$, the box $\mathcal B=[0,N)^2\times[N,2N)\cap\mathbb Z^3$, and the weight $W(A)$ from <a href="#aux:weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="aux:weight">[aux:weight]</a>. For a column $u\in\mathcal B$, its projection means $(u_1/u_3,u_2/u_3)$.

<a id="stmt-zero-weighted-zero"></a>
<div id="zero:weighted-zero" class="proposition">

<a id="zero:target"></a>
**Proposition 20**. *For the lattice $\Lambda$ and weight $W$ above, let $\mathcal Z$ be the set of integer $3\times3$ matrices whose columns belong to $\mathcal B$, whose rows belong to $\Lambda$, whose determinant is zero, and whose three projected columns are pairwise distinct. Then $$\label{zero:target}
 \sum_{A\in\mathcal Z}W(A)
 \ll \log(2N)\frac{N^6}{I^2}.$$ The implied constant is independent of the fixed digit matrix $C$.*

</div>

The orbit restriction in Proposition <a href="#sam:weighted-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sam:weighted-count">18</a> only reduces this sum. We first record the lattice estimates needed for the null vectors.

<a id="stmt-zero-plane"></a>
<div id="zero:plane" class="lemma">

<a id="zero:row-count"></a>
**Lemma 21**. *For a primitive vector $x\in\mathbb Z^3$, put $$\Lambda_x=\Lambda\cap x^\perp,\qquad
 x\cdot\Lambda=g(x)\mathbb Z,\qquad
 J_x=\det(\Lambda_x),$$ where $g(x)>0$. Then <a id="zero:plane-data"></a>$$\label{zero:plane-data}
 g(x)\mid E,\qquad J_x=\frac{I|x|}{g(x)},\qquad
 \frac{g(x)^3}{I}\le h^2.$$ The reduction of $\Lambda_x$ modulo $q$ is the entire plane $$\Pi_x=\{v\in\mathbb F_q^3:v\cdot\bar x=0\},
 \qquad \bar x=x\bmod q\ne0.$$ If $\Gamma\subseteq\Lambda_x$ is a sublattice of index $m$, the number of ordered triples of vectors in $\Gamma$, all of length at most $4N$ and spanning a plane over $\mathbb Q$, is at most $$\label{zero:row-count}
 \ll \frac{N^6}{(mJ_x)^3}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-zero-plane"><summary>Proof of stmt-zero-plane</summary>

*Proof.* Primitivity gives $x\cdot\mathbb Z^3=\mathbb Z$. Since $E\mathbb Z^3\subseteq\Lambda$, we have $E\mathbb Z\subseteq g(x)\mathbb Z$, so $g(x)\mid E$. Lemma <a href="#lat:plane-covolume" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lat:plane-covolume">4</a> gives the formula for $J_x$, and $g(x)^3/I\le E^2/D\le h^2$.

Primitivity also implies $\bar x\ne0$, with no restriction on the size of $x$. To prove surjectivity, choose $z\in\mathbb Z^3$ with $x\cdot z=1$. If an integral lift $v$ of a point of $\Pi_x$ has $x\cdot v=qa$, then $v-qaz\in\mathbb Z^3\cap x^\perp$ is another lift of the same point. Thus $\mathbb Z^3\cap x^\perp$ reduces onto $\Pi_x$. Its multiple by $E$ lies in $\Lambda_x$ and still reduces onto $\Pi_x$, because $q\nmid E$.

Finally, a triple counted in <a href="#zero:row-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="zero:row-count">[zero:row-count]</a> contains two independent vectors of length at most $4N$. Therefore the second successive minimum of $\Gamma$ is at most $4N$ whenever the count is nonzero. Lemma <a href="#lat:plane-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lat:plane-count">3</a> then bounds the choices for each vector by $O(N^2/\det\Gamma)$, and $\det\Gamma=mJ_x$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-zero-moment"></a>
<div id="zero:moment" class="lemma">

**Lemma 22**. *For $R\ge h$, <a id="zero:moment-bound"></a>$$\label{zero:moment-bound}
 \sum_{\substack{x\in\mathbb Z^3\ \mathrm{primitive}\\
                  R\le |x|<2R}}g(x)^3
 \ll R^3 I.$$ In particular, this estimate holds when $R>H/2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-zero-moment"><summary>Proof of stmt-zero-moment</summary>

*Proof.* Since $g(x)\mid B^e$, write $g(x)=B^{a(x)}$ with $0\le a(x)\le e$. For $0\le j\le e$, the condition $B^j\mid g(x)$ is equivalent to $Cx=0\pmod{B^j}$. Indeed, if $\widetilde C$ is any integral lift of $C$, then $\Lambda$ is the sum of the integral row span of $\widetilde C$ and $h\mathbb Z^3$, and $B^j\mid h$. The diagonal form of $C$ shows that the fraction of residue classes modulo $B^j$ satisfying this congruence is $$B^{-j-\max(j-b,0)}.$$ Indeed, the first diagonal coordinate must vanish, the second must be divisible by $B^{\max(j-b,0)}$, and the third is unrestricted since $j\le e$.

Because $R\ge h\ge B^j$, each residue class contains $O((R/B^j)^3)$ integral vectors of length less than $2R$. We may discard primitivity for this upper bound. Consequently, $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{\substack{x\ \mathrm{primitive}\\R\le |x|<2R}}g(x)^3
 &\le \sum_{j=0}^e B^{3j}
       \#\{x\in\mathbb Z^3:|x|<2R,\ Cx=0\pmod{B^j}\}\\
 &\ll R^3\sum_{j=0}^e B^{2j-\max(j-b,0)}
 \ll R^3 B^{b+e}=R^3I.
\end{aligned}$$ The exponents in the last sum strictly increase to $b+e$, so its bound is uniform in $B$. Finally, $h\ge2$ implies $H/2=h^2/2\ge h$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-zero-moment-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-zero-moment</summary>

<a id="zero:null-range"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#zero:weighted-zero" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="zero:weighted-zero">20</a>.* Every $A\in\mathcal Z$ has rational rank two. Indeed, its columns are nonzero, and no two are proportional because their projections are distinct. Its right nullspace therefore has a primitive integral generator $x$, unique up to sign. Every coordinate of $x$ is nonzero: otherwise the relation would make two columns proportional. Taking the cross product of two independent rows and dividing by the gcd of its coordinates gives $$\label{zero:null-range}
 Ax=0,\qquad x_1x_2x_3\ne0,\qquad |x|\ll N^2.$$ Every row of $A$ lies in $\Lambda_x$ and has length at most $4N$. Thus $x$ records a relation among the columns, while the rows are lattice vectors perpendicular to $x$. We may sum over both choices of sign of $x$ for an upper bound.

<a id="zero:fixed-null-count"></a>
Partition the possible vectors into dyadic shells $R\le |x|<2R$, $R=1,2,4,\ldots$. By <a href="#zero:null-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="zero:null-range">[zero:null-range]</a>, there are $O(\log(2N))$ shells. We will bound the weighted contribution of each shell by $O(N^6/I^2)$. For a fixed primitive $x$ in such a shell, a condition confining every row to an index-$m$ sublattice of $\Lambda_x$ gives at most $$\label{zero:fixed-null-count}
 \ll \frac{N^6g(x)^3}{m^3I^3R^3}$$ matrices of rational rank two, by <a href="#zero:row-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="zero:row-count">[zero:row-count]</a> and $J_x=I|x|/g(x)$. In the affine case we will sum $g(x)^3$ using Lemma <a href="#zero:moment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="zero:moment">22</a>. In the equal-pair cases we instead use $g(x)^3\le Ih^2$, together with an extra row congruence or a smaller number of possible null vectors. The support statement and the three weight bounds in Proposition <a href="#aux:weights" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="aux:weights">15</a> give the following exhaustive cases.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
###### Affinely independent residues.

If $W(A)>0$ and the columns of $A\bmod q$ are affinely independent, Lemma <a href="#aux:random-set" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="aux:random-set">14</a> excludes the relation $Ax=0$ when $|x|\le H$. To see both parts of this exclusion, if $x_1+x_2+x_3\ne0$ use the restricted shift; if the sum is zero, use affine independence and $\bar x\ne0$. Hence a contributing shell satisfies $R>H/2$. Using $W(A)\ll1$, <a href="#zero:fixed-null-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="zero:fixed-null-count">[zero:fixed-null-count]</a> with $m=1$, and Lemma <a href="#zero:moment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="zero:moment">22</a>, its contribution is at most <a id="zero:affine-count"></a>$$\label{zero:affine-count}
 \ll \frac{N^6}{I^3R^3}
       \sum_{\substack{x\ \mathrm{primitive}\\R\le |x|<2R}}g(x)^3
 \ll \frac{N^6}{I^2}.$$

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
###### An equal pair with an additional equation.

Every other matrix of positive weight has two equal columns modulo $q$. Fix one pair $i<j$ with this property, and put $\delta=\mathbf e_i-\mathbf e_j$, where the $\mathbf e_i$ are the standard basis vectors. Summing over the three pairs only changes the implied constant. First suppose that $\bar x\notin\mathbb F_q\delta$. The functional $v\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \OriginalMapsto\EndAccSupp{}}v\cdot\delta$ is nonzero on $\Pi_x$: the annihilator of $\Pi_x$ is precisely $\mathbb F_q\bar x$. By Lemma <a href="#zero:plane" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="zero:plane">21</a>, the rows of $A$ therefore belong to the index-$q$ sublattice $$\{v\in\Lambda_x:v_i=v_j\pmod q\}.$$ There are $O(R^3)$ integral $x$ in any shell. Using <a href="#zero:fixed-null-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="zero:fixed-null-count">[zero:fixed-null-count]</a> with $m=q$, the pointwise bound $g(x)^3\le Ih^2$, and $W(A)\ll(q^3/s)^2$, the contribution is at most <a id="zero:equal-additional"></a>$$\label{zero:equal-additional}
 \ll \frac{N^6}{I^2}\,h^2q^{-3}(q^3/s)^2
 \ll \frac{N^6}{I^2}\,\frac{h^{26}}q
 \ll \frac{N^6}{I^2}.$$ Here and below we use $q^3/s\ll qH^6=qh^{12}$ and $q>h^{100}$. This argument includes matrices of every rank modulo $q$.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
###### An equal pair without an additional equation.

<a id="zero:special-normals"></a>
It remains to consider $\bar x\in\mathbb F_q\delta$. The coordinate of $x$ outside the pair $i,j$ is divisible by $q$ and, by <a href="#zero:null-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="zero:null-range">[zero:null-range]</a>, is a nonzero integer. Thus $|x|\ge q$, and a contributing shell has $R>q/2$. The nonzero multiples of $\delta$ give $q-1$ residue classes for $x$ modulo $q$. Each class contains $O((1+R/q)^3)$ vectors of length less than $2R$, so the number of possible $x$ in this shell is $$\label{zero:special-normals}
 \ll R^3/q^2.$$ **Rank at least two modulo $q$.** The weight bound $W(A)\ll q^3/s$ now gives <a id="zero:special-rank-two"></a>$$\label{zero:special-rank-two}
 \ll \frac{N^6}{I^2}\,h^2q^{-2}(q^3/s)
 \ll \frac{N^6}{I^2}\,\frac{h^{14}}q
 \ll \frac{N^6}{I^2}$$ for the contribution of the shell.

**Rank at most one modulo $q$.** All rows reduce into one of the $q+1$ one-dimensional linear subspaces of $\Pi_x$. This also covers the zero row space. For each such line $\ell\subseteq\Pi_x$, surjectivity in Lemma <a href="#zero:plane" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="zero:plane">21</a> shows that $$\{v\in\Lambda_x:v\bmod q\in\ell\}$$ has index $q$ in $\Lambda_x$. Formula <a href="#zero:row-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="zero:row-count">[zero:row-count]</a> thus bounds the rank-two rational row triples, summed over all these lines, by $$\ll(q+1)\frac{N^6}{(qJ_x)^3}
 \ll\frac{N^6}{q^2J_x^3}.$$ Using <a href="#zero:special-normals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="zero:special-normals">[zero:special-normals]</a> and the general weight bound, the weighted contribution is at most <a id="zero:special-rank-one"></a>$$\label{zero:special-rank-one}
 \ll \frac{N^6}{I^2}\,h^2q^{-4}(q^3/s)^2
 \ll \frac{N^6}{I^2}\,\frac{h^{26}}{q^2}
 \ll \frac{N^6}{I^2}.$$

The affine and equal-pair cases cover the support of $W$. Their bounds are uniform over the dyadic shells, so summing over the $O(\log(2N))$ shells proves <a href="#zero:target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="zero:target">[zero:target]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="alt:section"></a>
### Deletion and projective normalization

The preceding estimates allow us to remove every repeated projected point and every small triangle while retaining many columns. We first construct a set for each sufficiently large prime $r$, compare its area bound with its cardinality, and then pass to every sufficiently large cardinality. All constants below may depend on the fixed integer $k$; none depends on the growing prime $r$.

This final step is an elementary alteration argument. The earlier probabilistic lower bound of Komlós, Pintz and Szemerédi (Komlós, Pintz, and Szemerédi 1982) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[KPS1982](#ref-KPS1982)] uses a stronger hypergraph independence lemma; no such lemma is needed below.

<details id="proof-stmt-zero-moment-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-zero-moment</summary>

<a id="alt:expected-events"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#intro:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:main">1</a>.* Keep the parameters constructed above, with $d=41$, and set <a id="alt:sample-size"></a>$$\label{alt:sample-size}
 a_r=r\sqrt{N^3/\tau},\qquad n_r=\lfloor a_r\rfloor.$$ Here $k\ge2$ and $B$ is an odd prime, so $\tau\ge1$. Take $2n_r$ samples with the shared random choices and independent conditional draws specified in Section <a href="#sam:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sam:section">6</a>. Let $Z$ count the unordered pairs of sample indices with equal projected points, plus the unordered triples whose projected points are pairwise distinct and whose determinant has absolute value at most $\tau$. Linearity of expectation, Lemma <a href="#sam:proportional" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sam:proportional">16</a>, and Corollary <a href="#sam:bad-triple" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sam:bad-triple">19</a> give $$\label{alt:expected-events}
 \frac{\mathbb EZ}{n_r}
 \ll n_r(hq)^6N^{-3}
    +n_r^2(\log(2N))^2\frac{h}{N^3r^d}.$$ No independence between the counted events is needed.

Both terms tend to zero. Indeed, $N=(hq)^{10}$ and $\tau\ge1$ give $$n_r(hq)^6N^{-3}\le r\tau^{-1/2}(hq)^{-9}=o(1).$$ For the other term, the definition of $n_r$ gives $$n_r^2(\log(2N))^2\frac{h}{N^3r^d}
 \le (\log(2N))^2\frac{h}{\tau}r^{2-d}.$$ Since $B^{k-1}\ge3$, the definition of $\tau$ implies $\tau\ge B^{k-1}/3$, and hence $h/\tau\le3B=O_k(r^{30})$. The bounds $B=O_k(r^{30})$, $q\le2h^{100}$, and $N=(hq)^{10}$ also give $\log(2N)=O_k(\log r)$. Thus the second term in <a href="#alt:expected-events" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alt:expected-events">[alt:expected-events]</a> is $O_k((\log r)^2r^{-9})=o(1)$. Thus $\mathbb EZ=o(n_r)$.

For sufficiently large $r$, choose an outcome with $Z<n_r$. For each violating pair or triple in that outcome, mark one of its indices for deletion. This marks at most $Z$ indices and meets every original violation. After deleting all marked indices, at least $n_r$ remain; retain any $n_r$ of them. Their projections form a set $P_r$ of $n_r$ distinct points in $[0,1)^2$, and every triple of retained columns satisfies $|\det A|>\tau$. In particular, the construction excludes every zero-area triple as well.

<a id="alt:area-bound"></a>
<a id="alt:scaled-area"></a>
For a retained triple, divide the $j$th column of $A$ by its positive third coordinate $A_{3j}$. The resulting columns have the form $(p_{j1},p_{j2},1)^{\mathsf T}$, so the determinant formula for triangle area gives the exact identity <a id="alt:area-identity"></a>$$\label{alt:area-identity}
 \operatorname{Area}(p_1p_2p_3)
 =\frac{|\det A|}{2A_{31}A_{32}A_{33}}.$$ Since $N\le A_{3j}<2N$ for each $j$, every retained triangle has area at least $\tau/(16N^3)$. Consequently $$\label{alt:area-bound}
 \Delta(P_r)\ge\frac{\tau}{16N^3}.$$ Also $\tau<h$ and $N\ge h^{10}$ give $a_r>rh^{29/2}\to\infty$. Hence $n_r\to\infty$ and eventually $n_r\ge a_r/2$. Combining this with <a href="#alt:area-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alt:area-bound">[alt:area-bound]</a> yields $$\label{alt:scaled-area}
 n_r^2\Delta(P_r)\ge\frac{r^2}{64}.$$

<a id="alt:cardinality-growth"></a>
<a id="alt:exponent"></a>
<a id="alt:prime-area"></a>
We now express both the cardinality and the area bound in terms of $r$. The parameter choices give $$\begin{gathered}
  100k^2r^{30}<B\le200k^2r^{30},\qquad h=B^k,\\
  h^{100}<q\le2h^{100},\qquad N^{3/2}=(hq)^{15},\\
  B^{k-1}/3\le\tau\le B^{k-1}/2.
 \end{gathered}$$ Consequently, if $$\beta=1515k-\frac{k-1}{2}=\frac{3029k+1}{2},$$ then $$\sqrt{2}\,rB^\beta<a_r\le2^{15}\sqrt{3}\,rB^\beta.$$ Since $a_r/2\le n_r\le a_r$ for sufficiently large $r$, there are fixed constants $A_k,D_k>0$ such that $$\label{alt:cardinality-growth}
 A_kr^\alpha\le n_r\le D_kr^\alpha,
 \qquad \alpha=1+30\beta=45435k+16,$$ for every sufficiently large prime $r$. In particular, put $$\label{alt:exponent}
 \eta=\frac2\alpha=\frac{2}{45435k+16}.$$ The upper bound in <a href="#alt:cardinality-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alt:cardinality-growth">[alt:cardinality-growth]</a> gives $r^2\ge D_k^{-\eta}n_r^\eta$. Combining this with <a href="#alt:scaled-area" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alt:scaled-area">[alt:scaled-area]</a>, we obtain $$\label{alt:prime-area}
 \Delta(P_r)\ge c_*n_r^{-2+\eta},
 \qquad c_*=\frac1{64D_k^\eta}>0.$$ Thus the same cardinality estimate that will permit interpolation also converts the factor $r^2$ into a fixed power of the number of points.

For a sufficiently large integer $n$, put $m=\lceil(n/A_k)^{1/\alpha}\rceil$. Lemma <a href="#norm:prime-interval" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="norm:prime-interval">8</a> supplies a prime $r$ with $m<r\le2m$. Once $(n/A_k)^{1/\alpha}\ge1$, we have $m\le2(n/A_k)^{1/\alpha}$, so <a href="#alt:cardinality-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alt:cardinality-growth">[alt:cardinality-growth]</a> gives $$n\le n_r\le C_kn,
 \qquad C_k=\max\left\{1,\frac{4^\alpha D_k}{A_k}\right\}.$$ This uses only the two-sided cardinality bounds, not monotonicity of $n_r$. Retain any $n$ points of $P_r$. Deleting points cannot decrease the minimum triangle area. Since $0<\eta<2$, <a href="#alt:prime-area" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alt:prime-area">[alt:prime-area]</a> therefore yields $$\Delta(n)\ge c_* n_r^{-2+\eta}
 \ge c_*C_k^{-2+\eta}n^{-2+\eta}.$$ Taking $c_1=c_*C_k^{-2+\eta}$ proves Theorem <a href="#intro:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:main">1</a>. All constants are absolute because $k$ is fixed independently of $r$ and $n$. The formula <a href="#alt:exponent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alt:exponent">[alt:exponent]</a> is explicit; no attempt has been made to optimize the construction parameters. ◻

</details>

<a id="app:prime-interval"></a>
### An elementary prime-interval estimate

We prove Lemma <a href="#norm:prime-interval" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="norm:prime-interval">8</a>, the form of Bertrand’s postulate used to choose the two moduli and to pass to every sufficiently large cardinality. The argument is the elementary binomial proof of Erdős (Erdős 1932) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Erdos1932](#ref-Erdos1932)].

<details id="proof-stmt-zero-moment-4"><summary>Proof of stmt-zero-moment</summary>

*Proof of Lemma <a href="#norm:prime-interval" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="norm:prime-interval">8</a>.* Write $P(x)=\prod_{p\le x}p$, where the product is over primes. First, $P(x)<4^x$ for $x\ge1$. It suffices to prove this for integer $x$ by induction. The cases $x=1,2$ are immediate. An even integer $x>2$ is composite, so $P(x)=P(x-1)$. For $x=2m+1$, every prime in $(m+1,2m+1]$ divides $\binom{2m+1}{m}$. The two central binomial coefficients are equal and their sum is strictly less than $2^{2m+1}$, whence $\binom{2m+1}{m}<4^m$. Thus $$P(2m+1)\le P(m+1)\binom{2m+1}{m}<4^{2m+1}.$$

Suppose now that there is no prime in $(n,2n]$. The largest binomial coefficient in $(1+1)^{2n}$ gives $\binom{2n}{n}\ge4^n/(2n+1)$. For any prime $p$, its exponent in this coefficient is $$\sum_{j\ge1}\left(\left\lfloor\frac{2n}{p^j}\right\rfloor
              -2\left\lfloor\frac{n}{p^j}\right\rfloor\right).$$ Each summand is zero or one, so the entire prime power contributed by $p$ is at most $2n$. The primes at most $\sqrt{2n}$ therefore contribute at most $(2n)^{\sqrt{2n}}$. Larger primes have exponent at most one. Those in $(2n/3,n]$ have exponent zero, and by assumption there are none in $(n,2n]$. For sufficiently large $n$ it follows that $$\frac{4^n}{2n+1}\le\binom{2n}{n}
 \le(2n)^{\sqrt{2n}}P(2n/3)
 <(2n)^{\sqrt{2n}}4^{2n/3}.$$ Taking logarithms contradicts $(n/3)\log4>\sqrt{2n}\log(2n)+\log(2n+1)$ for sufficiently large $n$. ◻

</details>
